In cases (ii)-(v)
we have
g\ntriangleleft f
.

2.

Let
f,g\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
. Then
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft f
if and only if
f\subseteq g
or
g\subseteq f
.

Proof.

Let us begin with the first statement.
It is clear that all five cases are distinct.
Suppose neither of (ii), (iii) (iv) and (v) occurs,
and we will seek to prove that (i) holds.
Since (ii) fails we have
g(t\star)\leq f(t\star)
for all
t\star\geq\sigma+
.
Since
f\vartriangleleft g
we also have
f(t\star)\leq g(t\star)
for all such
t\star
, hence in fact we have equality for
t\star\geq\sigma+
,
in particular at
t\star=\sigma+
.
Since (iii) fails,
f(t-)
and
g(t-)
lie (non-strictly) on the same side of
f(t+)=g(t+)
.

Consider first when they both lie to the left, that is
f(t-)\vee g(t-)\leq f(t+)=g(t+)
.
We divide into three cases
based upon whether
\sigma_{f}=\sigma_{g}
,
\sigma_{f}<\sigma_{g}
or
\sigma_{g}<\sigma_{f}
.

•

If
\sigma_{f}=\sigma_{g}
then
\sigma_{f}=\sigma_{g}=\sigma
, in which case (i) occurs.

•

If
\sigma_{f}<\sigma_{g}
then
\sigma_{g}=\sigma
, so we have

L_{\sigma-}(f)=[-\infty,f(\sigma-))
and
R_{\sigma-}(g)=(g(\sigma-),\infty]
.
By Lemma
3.1.2
we have
L_{\sigma-}(f)\cap R_{\sigma-}(g)=\emptyset
so
f(\sigma-)\leq g(\sigma-)
.
Hence
g\subseteq f
.

•

If
\sigma_{g}<\sigma_{f}
then
\sigma_{f}=\sigma
and as (iv) does not occur we must have
g(\sigma-)\leq f(\sigma-)
, which means
f\subseteq g
.

In all three cases we have that (i) occurs.
It remains to consider when both
f(\sigma-)
and
g(\sigma-)
lie to the right of
f(\sigma+)=g(\sigma+)
.
A symmetric argument,
in which (v) takes the place of (vi), shows that (i) also occurs.
Note that in case (ii) we have
L_{t\star}(f)\cap R_{t\star}(g)\neq\emptyset
.
In cases (iii), (vi) and (v) we have
L_{\sigma-}(g)\cap R_{\sigma-}(f)\neq\emptyset
.
Thus, in all of cases (ii)-(v) Lemma
3.1.2
gives
g\ntriangleleft f
.
For the second claim of the present lemma,
the reverse implication is trivial from Definition
3.1.1

and the forwards implication follows from part 1 of the present lemma.

\blacksquare

Part 1 of Lemma
3.1.5
makes explicit
the difficulties inherent to paths that may jump at their initial times.
We will often use arguments that
give some special attention to the initial times of paths.
The sets
L(f),R(g)
and the relation
\vartriangleleft

allow us to do so
without having to work through cases (i)–(v) in turn.

Lemma 3.1.6

Let
A\subseteq\Pi^{\uparrow}
be non-crossing.
Then
(A_{\max},\vartriangleleft)
is a totally ordered space.

Proof.

Recall that
A_{\max}
was defined in (
2.5
).
By Lemma
3.1.3
all pairs
f,g\in A
satisfy
f\vartriangleleft g
or
g\vartriangleleft f
,
which holds in particular for
A_{\max}
.
It is clear from Definition
3.1.1
that
f\vartriangleleft f
for all
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
,
thus also for all
f\in A_{\max}
.
If
f,g\in A_{\rm\max}
satisfy
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft f
,
then Lemma
3.1.5
tells us that
f\subseteq g
or
g\subseteq f
.
By maximality, this implies
f=g
.
We have now shown that
\vartriangleleft
is reflexive and antisymmetric,
and that all pairs of elements are comparable.
It remains to show that
\vartriangleleft
is transitive.

Let
f,g,h\in A_{\max}
with
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
.
If
f=g
or
g=h
then it is trivial that
f\vartriangleleft h
.
If
f=h
then we have
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h=f
, so Lemma
3.1.5

implies that
f\subseteq g
or
g\subseteq f
, which by maximality implies
f=g
, hence also
f\vartriangleleft g
.
Thus we may assume without loss of generality that
f,g,h
are distinct elements of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
By Lemma
3.1.2
we have
L(f)\cap R(g)=\emptyset
and
L(g)\cap R(h)=\emptyset
,
and we must show that
L(f)\cap R(h)
is also empty.

We will argue by contradiction.
Suppose that
(x,t\star)\in L(f)\cap R(h)
,
which implies that
L_{t\star}(f)=[-\infty,f(t\star))
,
R_{t\star}(h)=(h(t\star),\infty]
and
h(t\star)<x<f(t\star)
.
Consider first if
t\star\geq\sigma_{g}-
.
If
g(t\star)\leq x
then
g(t\star)\in L_{t\star}(f)
, Lemma
3.1.4
gives that
g\vartriangleleft f
,
in which case part 2 of Lemma
3.1.5
and maximality gives that
g=f
, which is a contradiction to our assumptions.
Similarly, if
g(t\star)\geq x
then
g(t\star)\in R_{t\star}(h)
, Lemma
3.1.4
gives that
g\vartriangleleft h
,
from which part 2 of Lemma
3.1.5
and maximality give
g=h
, which is again a contradiction.

It remains to consider when
t<\sigma_{g}
.
In this case
\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{h}<\sigma_{g}
.
Definition
3.1.1
thus implies that for all
s\star\geq\sigma_{g}+
we have
f(s\star)\leq g(s\star)\leq h(s\star)
.
Applying part 1 of Lemma
3.1.5
to
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
,
and noting that these are distinct maximal paths (so case (i) of that lemma may not occur),
we obtain that for some
s,s^{\prime}\geq\sigma_{g}
it holds that
f(s-)<g(s-)
and
g(s^{\prime}-)<h(s^{\prime}-)
.
If
s=s^{\prime}=\sigma_{g}
then
f(s-)<g(s-)<h(s-)
.
If
s>\sigma_{g}
then
f(s-)<g(s-)\leq h(s-)
,
similarly if
s^{\prime}>\sigma_{g}
then
f(s^{\prime}-)\leq g(s^{\prime}-)<h(s^{\prime}-)
.
In all three cases we have
u\geq\sigma
such that
f(u-)<h(u-)
.
We have
\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{h}<\sigma_{g}\leq s
, so
L_{u-}(h)=[-\infty,h(u-))
and
R_{u-}(f)=(f(u-),\infty]
,
meaning that
L(h)\cap R(f)\neq\emptyset
.
However now both
L(f)\cap R(h)
and
L(h)\cap R(f)
are non-empty,
which by Lemma
3.1.3
implies that
f
and
h
cross,
which is contradiction.

\blacksquare

Lemma 3.1.7

Let
A
be non-crossing and let
B
be non-crossing,
both subsets of
\Pi^{\uparrow}
.
Suppose
A\preceq B
.
Then
A\cup B
is non-crossing.

Proof.

Let
f\in A
,
g\in B
and assume that
A\preceq B
.
Since
A\subseteq B_{\uparrow}
there exists
h\in B_{\uparrow}
such that
f\subseteq h
.
We have that
g,h\in B
and
B
is non-crossing,
so
\{g,h\}
is non-crossing, thus also
\{f,h\}
is non-crossing.
The result follows.

\blacksquare

3.2
On partial orders of sets

For the duration of Section
3.2

let
(E,\leq)
denote a partially ordered set.
We now recall some standard notation associated to partial orders.
For
A\subseteq E
, we write

A_{\leq}:=\{e\in E:e\leq e^{\prime}\mbox{ for some }e^{\prime}\in A\}

for the
downset
of

A
. Note that
(A_{\leq})_{\leq}=A_{\leq}
.
The
upset

A_{\geq}
of
A
is defined in the same way as the downset
A_{\leq}
,
but for the reversed order.
A
maximal element
of a subset

A\subseteq E
is an element
e\in A
such that there exists no
e^{\prime}\in A
with

e<e^{\prime}
. We write
A_{\rm max}=\{e\in A:e\mbox{ is a maximal
element of }A\}
.
A
minimal element
is defined in the same way
but for the reversed order.
As usual, we write
e<e^{\prime}
if

e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e\neq e^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.2.1

For
A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
, we have specified in (
2.5
) that

A_{\max}
refers to the maximal elements of
(A,\subseteq)
.
In
(
2.6
)
we defined the set
A_{\uparrow}
to be the downset of
A\subseteq\Pi^{\uparrow}
in
(\Pi^{\uparrow},\subseteq)
.
In Remark
3.3.4
we will note that

A\subseteq(A_{\max})_{\downarrow}
when
A
is compact
i.e. each path in
A
extends to at least one element of
A_{\max}
.
Note that

B_{\downarrow}\subseteq\Pi^{\downarrow}
from (
2.15
) is the downset of
B
in
(\Pi^{\downarrow},\subseteq)
.

The following lemma puts the relation
\preceq
introduced
in (
2.7
) into a wider framework.
It is a natural concept that might have been studied elsewhere
but have not been able to locate a reference.
With slight abuse of notation we will briefly use the notation
\preceq
in the more abstract setting.
We noted in Section
2.3
that
\preceq
on
\Pi
is related to how efficiently paths cover space.
In the abstract setting there is a somewhat clearer interpretation.
Specifically, under
\leq
both of the following two operations will make a set
A\subseteq E
strictly increase:
inserting a new element to
A
that (once inserted) is a
\leq
-maximal element;
removing an existing element that (prior to removal) is not a
\leq
-maximal element.

Lemma 3.2.2

Let
(E,\leq)
be a partially ordered set.
For
A,B\subseteq E
, write
A\preceq B
to mean that
A_{\leq}\cap B\subseteq A\subseteq B_{\leq}
.
Then
\preceq
is a partial order on the set of all subsets of
E
.

Proof.

Clearly
A\preceq A
. Also,
A\preceq B\preceq A
implies

A\subseteq B_{\leq}\cap A\subseteq B
and by a symmetric argument also

B\subseteq A
, so to complete the proof we must show that the relation
\preceq

is transitive. The relations
A\preceq B\preceq C
say that

{\rm(i)}\ A_{\leq}\cap B\subseteq A,\quad{\rm(ii)}\ A\subseteq B_{\leq},\quad{\rm(iii)}\ B_{\leq}\cap C\subseteq B,\quad{\rm(iv)}\ B\subseteq C_{\leq}.

When we apply one of these facts we will indicate which
with a superscript above the corresponding
\subseteq
.
This implies (v)
A_{\leq}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm{(ii)}}}{{\subseteq}}B_{\leq}
, (vi)

B_{\leq}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm{(iv)}}}{{\subseteq}}C_{\leq}
, and (vii)

B_{\leq}\cap C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm{(iii)}}}{{\subseteq}}B_{\leq}\cap B
, from which we get

A_{\leq}\cap C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm{(v),(vii)}}}{{\subseteq}}A_{\leq}\cap B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm{(i)}}}{{\subseteq}}A\quad\mbox{and}\quad A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm{(ii)}}}{{\subseteq}}B_{\leq}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\rm{(vi)}}}{{\subseteq}}C_{\leq},

proving that
A\preceq C
.

\blacksquare

A set
A
is said to be
decreasing
if
A=A_{\leq}

and
increasing
if
A=A_{\geq}
.
If
A
and
B
are decreasing sets, then
A\preceq B
if and only if
A\subseteq B
.
The proof is trivial and is left to the reader.

3.3
On compatibility of order and topology

We now turn our attention to the interaction between orders and limits.
We have now introduced several partial orders related to càdlàg paths:
the ‘path extension’ order
\subseteq
on
\Pi
,
the ‘coverage efficiency’ order
\preceq
on
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
,
and the ‘leftwards of’ relation
\vartriangleleft
that was shown in Lemma
3.1.6
to be a total order on
A_{\max}
, when
A
is closed.
In general
\vartriangleleft
is not even a partial order.
With these situations in mind we make the following general definition.

Definition 3.3.1

Let
(E,\mathscr{T})
be a topological space.
We say that a binary relation
\leq
on
E
is
compatible

if
\big\{(e,f)\in E^{2}:e\leq f\big\}
is a closed
subset of
E^{2}
(in the product topology).
With mild abuse of notation we also apply this terminology to metric spaces
(E,d_{E})

where
d_{E}
is a metric generating the topology on
E
.

The point is that compatibility implies that if
e_{n}\rightarrow e
and
f_{n}\rightarrow f
with
e_{n}\leq f_{n}
,
all elements of
E
, then we may conclude that
e\leq f
.
We wish to study this notion in the three situations listed above,
but as per our comments above we do not always wish to assume (in particular, for
\vartriangleleft
)
that
(E,\leq)
is partially ordered.
Our first result in this direction is negative:

Remark 3.3.2

It is straightforward to see that
(\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow}),d_{\mathcal{K}(\Pi)},\preceq)
is not compatible.
For example,
let
f(t\pm)=0
with
\sigma_{f}=0
and let
g_{n}(t\pm)=1/n
with
\sigma_{g_{n}}=1
.
Then
\{f\}\prec\{f,g_{n}\}
and
\{f,g\}\prec\{f\}
.
Examples also exist where
A_{n}\prec B_{n}
but the limits of
(A_{n})
and
(B_{n})
are incomparable;
we leave this as an exercise for the reader.
From a purely abstract point of view,
the non-trivial interaction of
\preceq
with taking limits in
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})

is the main source of interesting structure within the space of weaves.

Lemma 3.3.3

The partial order
\subseteq
is compatible with
(\Pi,d_{\Pi})
.

Proof.

Proposition
2.2.1
gives that convergence in
d_{\Pi}
is equivalent
to convergence under the metric
(f,g)\mapsto d_{\mathcal{K}((\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c})^{2})}(H^{(2)}(f),H^{(2)}(g))
.
By definition (see Section
2.3
)
f\subseteq g
means that
H^{(2)}(f)\subseteq H^{(2)}(g)
.
With these facts in mind the stated result follows from Lemma
A.1.4
,
which asserts that the partial order of set inclusion is compatible with the Hausdorff metric.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.3.4

Using Lemma
3.3.3

it is straightforward to check that if
A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)

then for all
f\in A
there exists
g\in A_{\max}
such that
f\subseteq g
.
We will use this fact repeatedly, without referring back to this remark, from now on.

We now consider the relation
\vartriangleleft
, which will require rather more work.
The following lemma shows that any lack of compatibility between
\vartriangleleft
and
(\Pi^{\uparrow},d_{\Pi})

must involve a jump at the initial time of a limiting path.
The reader should bear in mind examples like

g_{n}(t\star)=\mathbbm{1}\{t\star\geq\frac{1}{n}+\}
,
g(t\star)=\mathbbm{1}\{t\star\geq 0+\}
,
f(t)=\mathbbm{1}\{t\star=0-\}
,
where
\sigma_{g_{n}}=\frac{1}{n}
and
\sigma_{f}=\sigma_{g}=0
.
Note that
f\vartriangleleft g_{n}
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
,
but
f
and
g
cross by jumping over each other in opposite directions at time
0
.
This example shows that
\vartriangleleft
is not compatible with
(\Pi^{\uparrow},d_{\Pi})
.

Lemma 3.3.5

Let
f,g,f_{n},g_{n}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
with
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
Write
\sigma_{n}=\sigma_{f_{n}}\vee\sigma_{g_{n}}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{g}
.
Suppose that

L_{s\bullet}(f_{n})\cap R_{s\bullet}(g_{n})=\emptyset

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\bullet\geq\sigma_{n}+
.
Then also

L_{s\bullet}(f)\cap R_{s\bullet}(g)=\emptyset

for all
s\bullet\geq\sigma+
.

Proof.

We will argue by contradiction.
Suppose that
L_{s\bullet}(f_{n})\cap R_{s\bullet}(g_{n})=\emptyset

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\bullet\geq\sigma_{n}+
,
and that
L_{t\star}(f)\cap R_{t\star}(g)\neq\emptyset

where
t\star\geq\sigma+
.
By the càdlàg property of
f
and
g
, without loss of generality we may take
t>\sigma

and assume that both
f
and
g
are continuous at
t
.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,t-\sigma)

and
N\in\mathbb{N}
be large enough that
|(\sigma_{f_{n}}\vee\sigma_{g_{n}})-\sigma|\leq\epsilon/2
for all
n\geq N
.
Our assumption that
L_{s\bullet}(f_{n})\cap R_{s\bullet}(g_{n})=\emptyset
implies that

f_{n}(s\bullet)\leq g_{n}(s\bullet)
for all
s\bullet\geq\sigma_{n}+
,
which in particular for
N\geq n
includes all
s\bullet\geq(t-\epsilon/2)+
.

Let
t_{n}\star_{n}\rightarrow t\star
.
It follows from Lemma
2.1.1

that
t_{n}\star_{n}\geq(\sigma\vee\sigma_{n})+
for all sufficiently large
n
,
so let us pass to a subsequence and assume that this holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Lemma
A.2.3
and continuity of
f,g
at
t
we have

f_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow f(t\star)
and
g_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow g(t\star)
.
As
g(t\star)<f(t\star)
we obtain that there exists
\delta>0
such that

g_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})\leq f_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})-\delta
.
For sufficiently large
n
we have
|t_{n}-t|<\epsilon/2
, which implies
t_{n}\star_{n}\geq(t-\epsilon/2)+
.
This contradicts the result of the previous paragraph.

\blacksquare

Lemma 3.3.6

The relation
\vartriangleleft
is compatible with
(\Pi^{\updownarrow},d_{\Pi})
.
Moreover: let
f_{n},g_{n},f,g\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
with
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
If
\{f_{n},g_{n}\}
is non-crossing for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\{f,g\}
is non-crossing.

Proof.

The first claim follows from Lemmas
3.3.5
and
3.1.2
,
noting that for
f,g\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}

the relation
f\vartriangleleft g
is equivalence to requiring that

L_{s+}(f)\cap R_{s+}(g)=\emptyset
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
For the second claim,
by part 2 of Lemma
3.1.5

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

we have that
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
or
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
.
At least one of these two possibilities must hold for infinitely many
n
.
From what we have already proved, it follows that
f\vartriangleleft g
or
g\vartriangleleft f
,
from which Lemma
3.1.3
gives that
f
and
g
do not cross.

\blacksquare

Lemma 3.3.7

The relation
\vartriangleleft
is compatible with
(\Pi^{\uparrow}_{c},d_{\Pi})
.
Moreover: let
f_{n},g_{n},f,g\in\Pi^{\uparrow}_{c}
with
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
If
\{f_{n},g_{n}\}
is non-crossing for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\{f,g\}
is non-crossing.

Proof.

The first claim follows from Lemmas
3.3.5
and
3.1.2
,
noting all
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}_{c}
satisfy
f(\sigma_{f}-)=f(\sigma_{f}+)
so that
L_{\sigma_{f}-}(f)=R_{\sigma_{f}-}(f)=\emptyset
.
The proof of the second claim is essentially the same as that of Lemma
3.3.6
.

\blacksquare

The remainder of this section concerns conditions under which
\vartriangleleft

is preserved in limits
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
with
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
for
half-infinite càdlàg paths.
We will see, in Lemma
3.3.10
that
the key (extra) condition is that
\{f,g\}
must be non-crossing.
From Lemma
3.3.5
if any crossing is too occur in such a limit,
it must take place at time
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{g}
.
It is helpful to introduce another relation,
which quantifies the ‘amount that
f
and
g
cross by at
\sigma
’,
whilst
f
and
g
are otherwise non-crossing.

Definition 3.3.8

Let
\epsilon>0
.
Let
f,g\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
and write
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{g}
.
We write
f\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g
if

\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and

L_{s\bullet}(f)\cap R_{s\bullet}(g)=\emptyset

for all
s\bullet\geq\sigma+
,
as well as

g(\sigma-)+\epsilon\leq f(\sigma-)
and

f(\sigma+)+\epsilon\leq g(\sigma+)
, with

g(\sigma-)+\epsilon\leq g(\sigma+)
and

f(\sigma+)+\epsilon\leq f(\sigma-)
.

Lemma 3.3.9

Let
f,g\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
and
let
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{g}
.

1.

If
f\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g
for some
\epsilon>0

then
f
and
g
cross.

2.

Suppose that
f_{n},g_{n}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
with
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
If
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
for all
n
and
f\ntriangleleft g
then
there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
f\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g
.

Proof.

We prove the two claims in turn.
For the first, suppose that
f\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g
.
Then
g(\sigma-)<g(\sigma+)
,
f(\sigma+)<f(\sigma-)

and
g(\sigma-)<f(\sigma-)
.
Hence
L_{\sigma-}(f)\cap R_{\sigma-}(g)\neq\emptyset
.
Since
f(\sigma+)<g(\sigma+)
we have
L_{\sigma+}(g)\cap R_{\sigma+}(f)\neq\emptyset
.
By Lemma
3.1.3
,
f
and
g
cross.

For the second claim,
let
f_{n}\rightarrow f
,
g_{n}\rightarrow g
with
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
and
f\ntriangleleft g
.
From Lemma
3.3.5
we have

L_{t\star}(f)\cap R_{t\star}(g)=\emptyset
and
f(t\star)\leq g(t\star)
for all
t\star\geq\sigma+
.
Since
f\ntriangleleft g
, by Lemma
3.1.2
we must have
L(f)\cap R(g)\neq\emptyset
,
which implies that
L_{\sigma-}(f)\cap R_{\sigma-}(g)\neq\emptyset
.
Therefore
L_{s-}(f)=[-\infty,f(\sigma-))
,
R_{\sigma-}(g)=(g(\sigma-),\infty]

with
g(\sigma-)<f(\sigma-)
.

If
g(\sigma+)\leq g(\sigma-)
then

f(\sigma+)\leq g(\sigma+)\leq g(\sigma-)<f(\sigma-)

which implies
f\vartriangleleft g
, so this may not occur.
Similarly, if
f(\sigma-)\leq f(\sigma+)
then
g(\sigma-)<f(\sigma-)\leq f(\sigma+)\leq g(\sigma+)
,
which implies
f\vartriangleleft g
, so this may not occur either.
Thus
g(\sigma-)<g(\sigma+)
and
f(\sigma+)<f(\sigma-)
.

It remains only to show that
f(\sigma+)<g(\sigma+)
.
Recall that we have
f(\sigma+)\leq g(\sigma+)
,
so we need only eliminate the case
f(\sigma+)=g(\sigma+)
.
We will argue by contradiction.
We thus assume
g(\sigma-)<g(\sigma+)=f(\sigma+)<f(\sigma-)
.
Our strategy is to show that compactness must fail for
(f_{n})
or
(g_{n})
,
because in avoiding crossing each other the paths
f_{n}
and
g_{n}

must become too erratic in a short time interval near
\sigma
.

Let

\kappa=\min\{g(\sigma+)-g(\sigma-),f(\sigma-)-f(\sigma+)\}

(3.2)

By right continuity of
f
and
g
at
\sigma+
,
there exists
\delta>0
such that

|f(t\star)-f(\sigma+)|\vee|g(t\star)-g(\sigma+)|\leq\kappa/4\qquad\text{ for all }t\star\in[\sigma+,(\sigma+\delta)+].

(3.3)

By Lemma
A.2.4
there exists

s_{n}\bullet_{n}
and
t_{n}\star_{n}
such that

s_{n}\rightarrow\sigma
,
t_{n}\rightarrow\sigma
and

f_{n}(s_{n}\bullet_{n})\rightarrow f(\sigma-)
and
g_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow g(\sigma-)
.
Without loss of generality
(or consider the following argument with the roles of
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
swapped)
we may assume that

s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq t_{n}\star_{n}
for infinite many
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
and let us pass to a subsequence upon which

s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq t_{n}\star_{n}
holds for all
n
.
In particular,
\sigma_{n}-\leq s_{n}\bullet_{n}
.
Without loss of generality we may pass to a further subsequence and assume that

s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq t_{n}\star_{n}\leq(\sigma+\delta/3)+

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
u=\sigma+\delta.

By Lemma
A.2.4
there exists

u_{n}\diamond_{n}
such that
u_{n}\rightarrow u
and
f_{n}(u_{n}\diamond_{n})\rightarrow f(u+)
.
Without loss of generality we may pass to a further subsequence and assume that

u_{n}\geq\sigma+2\delta/3
,
which implies that

s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq t_{n}\star_{n}<u_{n}\diamond_{n}.

(3.4)

Again, without loss of generality we may pass to a further subsequence and assume that

\displaystyle|f_{n}(s_{n}\bullet_{n})-f(\sigma-)|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa/4

\displaystyle|g_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})-g(\sigma-)|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa/4

\displaystyle|f_{n}(u_{n}\diamond_{n})-f(u+)|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa/4

for all
n
. It follows from the above equations,
(
3.2
) and (
3.3
) that

\displaystyle f_{n}(s_{n}\bullet_{n})

\displaystyle\geq f(\sigma+)+3\kappa/4

(3.5)

\displaystyle g_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})

\displaystyle\leq f(\sigma+)-3\kappa/4

(3.6)

\displaystyle|f_{n}(u_{n}\diamond_{n})-f(\sigma+)|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa/2

(3.7)

for all
n
.

We must now briefly divide into two cases.
If
g_{n}(t_{n}-)<g_{n}(t_{n}+)
then

L_{t_{n}-}(g_{n})=[-\infty,g_{n}(t_{n}-))

and by Lemma
3.1.4
we have

f_{n}(t_{n}-)\leq g_{n}(t_{n}-)<g_{n}(t_{n}+)
.
Alternatively, if
g_{n}(t_{n}+)\leq g_{n}(t_{n}-)
then we have

f_{n}(t_{n}+)\leq g_{n}(t_{n}+)<g_{n}(t_{n}-)
.
In either case we have
\circ\in\{-,+\}
such that

f_{n}(t_{n}\circ_{n})\leq g_{n}(t_{n}-)\wedge g_{n}(t_{n}+)\leq g_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n}).

From
(
3.6
) we thus obtain

f_{n}(t_{n}\circ_{n})\leq f(\sigma+)-3\kappa/4.

(3.8)

We must again briefly divide into two cases.
If
\star_{n}=-
then
(
3.4
)
gives
s_{n}<t_{n}
, so trivially
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq t_{n}\circ_{n}\leq u_{n}\diamond_{n}
.
Alternatively, if
\star_{n}=+
then
we have
f_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})\leq g_{n}(t_{n}\star_{n})
,
which from (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) we have
that
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\neq t_{n}\star_{n}
.
From (
3.4
) we thus have
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq t_{n}-
,
so in this case too we obtain that

s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq t_{n}\circ_{n}<u_{n}\diamond_{n}.

(3.9)

From Proposition
A.2.1
and
(
3.5
),
(
3.7
),
(
3.8
),
(
3.9
)
we obtain that the sequence
(f_{n})
is not relatively compact.
This is a contradiction,
and completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 3.3.10

If
A\subseteq\Pi^{\uparrow}
is non-crossing and closed then the relation
\vartriangleleft
is compatible with
(A,d_{\Pi})
.
Moreover: suppose that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
, where
f,g,f_{n},g_{n}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
and
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
for all
n
.
If
f
and
g
do not cross each other then
f\vartriangleleft g
.

Proof.

Note that the second claim is a stronger statement than the first.
The second claim permits paths within the sequence
(f_{n})
to cross each other,
and paths within the sequence
(g_{n})
to cross each other, requiring only that
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We will prove the second claim.
Let
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
, where
f,g,f_{n},g_{n}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
and
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
for all
n
.
By (both parts of) Lemma
3.3.9
, if
f\ntriangleleft g
then
f
and
g
cross.
The result follows.

\blacksquare

We commented below Definition
3.1.1
that
the relation
\vartriangleleft
is (in general) not a partial order.
In Lemma
3.1.6
we showed that
\vartriangleleft
is a total order on
A_{\max}
, provided
A\subseteq\Pi^{\uparrow}
is non-crossing.
However, the set
A_{\max}
is typically not a closed subset of
\Pi^{\uparrow}
,
even if
A
is a closed subset of
\Pi^{\uparrow}
.
For example, consider when
A
contains the paths
f(t)=k
for
k\in[0,1]
and
t\geq\sigma_{f}=0
,
plus the single path
g(t)=0
for
t\geq\sigma_{g}=-1
.
For this reason Lemmas
3.1.6
and
3.3.10
are both important to us,
but we must take care when using them together.
The following technical lemma will be used in Section
5.1

to help prove that
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
is measurable.

Lemma 3.3.11

Let
\epsilon>0
.
Suppose that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
,
where
f,g,f_{n},g_{n}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
,
with
\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{g}\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
f_{n}\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g_{n}
for all
n
then
f\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g
.

Proof.

We remark that the condition
\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{g}\in\mathbb{R}
is necessary,
in fact this is all that prevents
\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}

from being compatible with
(\Pi^{\uparrow},d_{\Pi})
.
Let
f,g,f_{n},g_{n}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
be as given and
write
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{g}
.
Suppose that
f_{n}\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g_{n}
for all
n
.
Lemma
3.3.5
gives that

L_{s\bullet}(f)\cap R_{s\bullet}(g)=\emptyset
for all
s\bullet\geq\sigma+
.
Noting that
\sigma_{n}\rightarrow s
, for all sufficiently large
n
we have
\sigma_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let us pass to a subsequence and assume that
\sigma_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
for all
n
.

We have
g_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)+\epsilon\leq g_{n}(\sigma_{n}+)
.
It follows from Lemma
A.2.2
that

g(\sigma-)+\epsilon\leq g(\sigma+)
.
Similarly, it follows from

f_{n}(\sigma_{n}+)+\epsilon\leq f_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)
that

f(\sigma+)+\epsilon\leq f(\sigma-)
.
From
g_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)+\epsilon\leq f_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)
we obtain

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}g_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)+\epsilon\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(\sigma_{n}-).

(3.10)

By Lemma
A.2.2
we have that

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}g_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)
lies between
g(\sigma-)
and
g(\sigma+)
.
We have already shown that
g(\sigma-)<g(\sigma+)
,
so in fact

g(\sigma-)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}g_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)
.
Similarly, Lemma
A.2.2
gives that

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)
lies between
f(\sigma-)
and
f(\sigma+)
.
We have already shown that
f(\sigma+)<f(\sigma-)
,
so in fact

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(\sigma_{n}-)\leq f(\sigma-)
.
From these facts and (
3.10
) we obtain

g(\sigma-)+\epsilon\leq f(\sigma-)
.

By compactness and Lemma
A.2.3

the sequence
(f_{n}(\sigma_{n}+))
has a limit point
a
between
f(\sigma-)
and
f(\sigma+)
.
We have shown that
f
jumps leftwards at
\sigma
, thus
f(\sigma+)\leq a
.
Similarly,
(g_{n}(\sigma_{n}+))
has a limit point
b
between
g(\sigma-)
and
g(\sigma+)
.
We have shown that
g
jumps rightwards at
\sigma
, thus
b\leq g(\sigma+)
.
Using that
f_{n}(\sigma_{n})+\epsilon\leq g_{n}(\sigma_{n}+)
we obtain that

a+\epsilon\leq b
, hence
f(\sigma+)+\epsilon\leq g(\sigma+)
.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

4
Deterministic weaves

Recall the space
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
introduced in (
2.11
).
An element of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
is known as a
deterministic weave
and is,
by definition, a deterministic element of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
that is pervasive and non-crossing.
We study deterministic weaves in this section,
although some results will involve probability within their proofs.
The results in this section will feed into the proofs of our main results, in Section
5
.
Our long term strategy is to establish what can be said in general about the internal structure of deterministic weaves,
to translate this information into statements about the geometric structure of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
,
and finally lift such results into
\mathscr{W}
.

Definition
2.4.1
defines webs and flows as, respectively, minimal and maximal elements of the space of weaves
\mathscr{W}
under
\preceq_{\rm d}
.
Recall that elements of
\mathscr{W}
are formally
probability measures on
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
.
We identify
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
with the subset of
\mathscr{W}

consisting of point-mass measures.
It is not immediately clear what Definition
2.4.1
means for deterministic weaves:
extremal points of
(\mathscr{W}_{\det},\preceq)

are not a priori extremal points of
(\mathscr{W},\preceq_{\rm d})
, nor vice versa
We will resolve these difficulties in Lemma
5.2.4
,
which shows that
a random weave
\mathcal{W}
is a web (resp. flow) if and only if

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{W}\text{ is almost surely minimal (resp.~maximal) in }(\mathcal{W}_{\det},\preceq)\right]=1
.
The proof of Lemma
5.2.4
will rely on key results established in Section
4
.
Therefore, until we have proved Lemma
5.2.4
we will avoid calling any deterministic or random elements of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
a ‘web’ or ‘flow’.

However, we will use the deterministic maps

A\mapsto\web_{D}(A)
and
A\mapsto\flow(A)

defined in (
2.12
) and (
2.13
)
from this point on.
The meaning of these maps on
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
is clear,
where in the former case
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is also taken to be deterministic.

4.1
Weaves and the non-crossing property

Weaves provide a structure inside of which càdlàg paths behave rather better than within arbitrary subsets of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
.
We remark that if
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
does not cross a weave
\mathcal{A}

then it is trivial to see that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{f\}
is also a weave.
Combining these two facts,
to some extent weaves are able to control the behaviour of paths that do not cross them.
We begin to explore this idea within the present section.
We start with a key technical lemma that uses all of the defining properties of weaves:
compactness, pervasiveness and the non-crossing property.
It captures what happens when we approximate the middle of a jump with paths beginning earlier in time.

Lemma 4.1.1

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave
and let
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
be a path that does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.

1.

Suppose
f(t-)<f(t+)
.
Then
there exists
h\in\mathcal{A}

such that
h(t-)\leq f(t-)
and
f\vartriangleleft h
.

2.

Suppose
f(t+)<f(t-)
.
Then
there exists
h\in\mathcal{A}

such that
f(t-)\leq h(t-)
and
h\vartriangleleft f
.

Proof.

The second statement follows from the first by considering space reflected about the origin
(and is written out in full for clarity)
so we will prove only the first statement.
Suppose
f(t-)<f(t+)
.
We will now argue that it suffices to prove that

\text{for any }x\in(f(t-),f(t+)\text{ there exists }h\in\mathcal{A}\text{ such that }h(t-)\leq f(t-)\text{ and }f\vartriangleleft h.

(4.1)

With (
4.1
) in hand,
let us write
h^{(x)}
for the path
h
generated from
x
,
and
note that compactness of
\mathcal{A}
implies the existence of a subsequential limit

h^{(x)}\rightarrow h^{\prime}
as
x\searrow f(t-)
.
As
f\vartriangleleft h^{(x)}
we have
h^{(x)}(t-)\leq f(t-)<f(t+)\leq h^{(x)}(t+)
,
so
Lemma
A.2.2
ensures that
h^{\prime}(t-)\leq f(t-)
.
Lemma
3.3.10
ensures that
f\vartriangleleft h^{\prime}
.
Thus
h^{\prime}
has the desired properties.

It remains to establish (
4.1
).
Let
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
f(t-)<x<f(t+)
,
and suppose that
f
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
(a_{n})\subseteq(0,\infty)
be such that
a_{n}\rightarrow 0
.
Let
z_{n}=(x,t-a_{n})
and by pervasiveness let
h_{n}\in\mathcal{A}(z_{n})
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{A}
, pass to a subsequence and assume without loss of generality that
h_{n}\rightarrow h\in\mathcal{A}
.
By Lemma
A.2.3
we have

h(t-)\wedge h(t+)\leq x\leq h(t-)\vee h(t+).

(4.2)

We have that
f
and
h_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
do not cross.
By Lemma
3.1.3
this means that
f\vartriangleleft h_{n}
or
h_{n}\vartriangleleft f
.
We now consider two cases.

Consider first if
f\vartriangleleft h_{n}
for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then Lemma
3.3.10
implies that
f\vartriangleleft h
.
In this case
f(t+)\leq h(t+)
, so
x<h(t+)
which by (
4.2
) implies
h(t-)\leq x^{\prime}
.
Hence
h(t-)\leq y
, and we have established all the required properties of
h
.

If the above case does not occur then there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
h_{n}\vartriangleleft f
for all
n\geq N
.
Without loss of generality we may pass to a subsequence and assume
h_{n}\vartriangleleft f
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
As
\sigma_{h_{m}}<t
and
L_{t-}(f)=[-\infty,f(t-))
,
Lemma
3.1.4

implies that
h_{n}(t-)\leq f(t-)
.
We have also that
h_{n}((t-a_{n})-)\vee h_{n}((t-a_{n})+)\geq x
.
Clearly
(t-a_{n})\pm<t-
for all
n
.
Hence by Lemma
A.2.3

we have

h(t-)\geq x>f(t-)\geq h(t+),

which as
f(t-)<f(t+)
means that
f
and
h
cross (by Definition
2.3.1
).
This is a contradiction, so in fact this case does not occur.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.1.2

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Let
f,h\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
be such that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{f\}
is non-crossing, and
\mathcal{A}\cup\{h\}
is non-crossing.
Let
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{h}
.
Suppose that
f(t\star)<h(t\star)
for some
t\star\geq\sigma+
.
Then for all
\epsilon>0

there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}
with
\sigma_{g}\leq t+\epsilon
such that
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
.

Further, we may choose
g
such that
g\nsubseteq f
,
f\nsubseteq g
,
g\nsubseteq h
and
h\nsubseteq g
.

Proof.

By the càdlàg property of
f
and
h

for any
\epsilon>0
there exists
t^{\prime}
such that
|t-t^{\prime}|<\epsilon/2
and
f(t^{\prime}+)<h(t^{\prime}+)
.
Hence we may choose
\epsilon>0

chosen sufficiently small that
f(s\pm)<h(s\pm)
for all
s\in[t^{\prime},t^{\prime}+\epsilon/2)
,
with
|t-t^{\prime}|<\epsilon/2
and
t^{\prime}>\sigma
.
By the càdlàg property of
f
and
h
,
choose
s\in(t^{\prime},t^{\prime}+\epsilon)
such that both
f
and
h
are continuous at
s
.
Note that
s\leq t+\epsilon
.
Recall that when
f
is continuous at
s
we write
f(s)=f(s\pm)
.
Let
x\in(f(s),h(s))
and by pervasiveness of
\mathcal{A}
let
g\in\mathcal{A}(x,s)
.
Hence
g(s-)\wedge g(s+)\leq x\leq g(s-)\vee g(s+)
.
By continuity of
f
at
s
we have
L_{s-}(f)=L_{s+}(f)=[-\infty,f(s))
.
Note that
f(s)<h(s)
implies that at least one of
g(s-)
and
g(s+)
is strictly greater than
f(t\star)
.
By Lemma
3.1.4
we thus have
f\vartriangleleft g
,
and as
\sigma_{f}<s
this means
f\nsubseteq g
and
g\nsubseteq f
.
A symmetrical argument (reflect space about the origin) shows that
g\vartriangleleft h
,
with
g\nsubseteq h
and
h\nsubseteq g
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.1.3

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\uparrow}
be a deterministic weave. If
B,C\subseteq\Pi^{\uparrow}
are such that
\mathcal{A}\cup B
is non-crossing, and
\mathcal{A}\cup C
is non-crossing,
then
\mathcal{A}\cup B\cup C
is non-crossing.

Proof.

Note that our conditions imply that
B
is non-crossing, and
C
is non-crossing.
It suffices to prove the case where
\mathcal{B}=\{f\}
and
\mathcal{C}=\{h\}
are singletons,
from which the general case follows immediately.
To this end, suppose that
f,g\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
are such that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{f\}
is non-crossing and
\mathcal{A}\cup\{h\}
is non-crossing.

We will argue by contradiction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{f\}\cup\{h\}
contains a pair of paths that cross.
From our assumptions, the only possibility is that

f
and
h
cross.
By Lemma
3.1.3

f
and
h
cross if and only if
L(g)\cap R(f)\neq\emptyset
and
L(f)\cap R(h)\neq\emptyset
.
Hence there exists
t\star,s\bullet\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
such that

t\star<s\bullet
, with

\displaystyle L_{t\star}(h)

\displaystyle=[-\infty,h(t\star)),\qquad R_{t\star}(f)

\displaystyle=(f(t\star),\infty],

\displaystyle L_{s\bullet}(f)

\displaystyle=[-\infty,f(s\bullet)),\qquad R_{s\bullet}(h)

\displaystyle=(h(s\bullet),\infty],

f(t\star)<h(t\star)
and
h(s\bullet)<f(s\bullet)
.
Let
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{h}
.

Consider, first, if
t\star\geq\sigma+
.
In this case,
Lemma
4.1.2
implies that there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}

such that
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
, with
\sigma_{g}<s\bullet
.
As
s\bullet\geq\sigma+
this means
f(s\bullet)\leq g(s\bullet)\leq h(s\bullet)
,
which is a contradiction to
h(s\bullet)<f(s\bullet)
.

It remains to consider the case
t\star=\sigma-
.
In this case
\sigma_{f}=\sigma
or
\sigma_{h}=\sigma
.
Without loss of generality (or consider space reflected about the origin)
let us assume that
\sigma_{f}=\sigma
.
As
R_{\sigma-}=(f(\sigma-),\infty]

and
\sigma_{f}=\sigma
it follows from (
3.1
) that

f(\sigma-)<f(\sigma+)
.
Lemma
4.1.1
implies the existence of
g\in\mathcal{A}

such that
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g(\sigma-)<f(\sigma+)
.
Hence
g(\sigma-)<h(\sigma-)
.
If
\sigma_{h}<\sigma
then it follows immediately
by Lemma
3.1.4
that
g\vartriangleleft h
.
Alternatively,
if
\sigma_{h}=s
then
L_{t\star}(h)=[-\infty,h(\sigma-))

so in this case too we have
g\vartriangleleft h
.
We now have
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
.
As
s\bullet\geq\sigma+
this means
f(s\bullet)\leq g(s\bullet)\leq h(s\bullet)
,
which is a contradiction to
h(s\bullet)<f(s\bullet)
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.1.4

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be deterministic weaves and suppose that
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing.
Let
C\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
be non-crossing.
Then
\mathcal{A}\cup C
is non-crossing if and only if
\mathcal{B}\cup C
is non-crossing.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case
C=\{f\}
where
f\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
,
from which the general case follows immediately.
Assume that
f\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
does not cross
{\cal A}
.
Let
b\in{\cal B}
.
By assumption
b
does not cross
{\cal A}
.
By Lemma
4.1.3
, we have that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{b\}\cup\{f\}
is non-crossing,
so in particular
f
and
b
do not cross each other.
Since
b\in{\cal B}
was arbitrary,
f
does not cross
\mathcal{B}
.

\blacksquare

In the proof of Lemma
4.1.3
we saw that
Lemma
4.1.2

was a natural counterpart to Lemma
4.1.1
.
The underlying principle is as follows.
If two paths
f
and
g
are such that
f\nsubseteq g
and
g\nsubseteq f
then either:

f(t+)\neq g(t+)
for some
t
,
or at least one of
f
and
g
has made a jump at its initial time,
in a direction away from the other.
Lemma
4.1.2
applied to the former case,
Lemma
4.1.1
to the latter,
resulting in a path
h
that lay between
f
and
g
.
Variations upon this theme will feature in the proof of several future results,
including the next lemma.

The following lemma is stated for paths
f,h\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
that do not cross a weave
\mathcal{A}
,
but at this stage it is perhaps best understood by considering the special case
f,h\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
Note that for maximal paths,
the condition
f\nsubseteq h
and
h\nsubseteq f
is simply the requirement that
f\neq h
.
In this case Lemma
4.1.5
provides a key piece of information about the geometric structure of
(\mathcal{A}_{\max},\vartriangleleft)
,
namely that any two distinct points, within the total order, will always have another point strictly in between them.
This lemma will be a key tool in Section
4.3
.

Lemma 4.1.5

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Suppose
f,h\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
do not cross
\mathcal{A}
,
with
f\vartriangleleft h
,
f\nsubseteq h
and
h\nsubseteq f
.
Then there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
such that
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
,
and
f\nsubseteq g
,
g\nsubseteq f
,
g\nsubseteq h
,
h\nsubseteq g
.

Proof.

Let
f,h
be as given in the lemma and set
\sigma=\sigma_{f}\vee\sigma_{h}
.
By part 2 of Lemma
3.1.5

our conditions on
f
and
h
imply
that
h\ntriangleleft f
.
From Lemma
3.1.2
we thus have

L(h)\cap R(f)\neq\emptyset
.
In particular there exists
t\star\geq\sigma-
such that

L_{t\star}(h)=[-\infty,h(t\star))
and
R_{t\star}(f)=(f(t\star),\infty]
with

f(t\star)<h(t\star)
.

Consider first if
t\star\geq\sigma+
. Then Lemma
4.1.2
implies the existence of

g\in\mathcal{A}
with
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft f
,
also
f\nsubseteq g
,
g\nsubseteq f
,
g\nsubseteq h
and
h\nsubseteq g
.
Without loss of generality we may take
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
,
which completes the proof in this case.

It remains to consider the case
t\star=\sigma-
.
In this case we have
\sigma=\sigma_{f}
or
\sigma=\sigma_{h}
.
Without loss of generality let us assume that
\sigma=s_{f}

(or consider space reflected about the origin).
As
R_{\sigma-}(f)
is non-empty this implies that
f(t-)<f(t+)
.

Remark 4.1.6

Let us briefly comment on the strategy for the remainder of the proof.
Although
f
jumps at
\sigma
, Lemma
4.1.1
is not suitable for use here
because (if used to construct
g
) it allows the possibility that
g\subseteq f
.
Instead, we require a more sophisticated version of the approximation scheme
used in the proof of Lemma
4.1.1
,
but the path we are looking for here is
not
the limiting path;
rather it is some path that occurs sufficiently close to the limit.
We require a path with several different properties.
To find it, we will repeatedly show that one such desired property can fail only for finitely many
n
,
then (without loss of generality) pass to a subsequence on which the property holds for all
n
.

As
t\star=\sigma-
we have
f(\sigma-)<h(\sigma-)
, so

f(\sigma-)\;<\;f(\sigma+)\wedge h(\sigma-).

(4.3)

Let
\epsilon>0
be such that
f(\sigma-)+2\epsilon\leq f(\sigma+)\wedge h(\sigma-)

and let
(a_{n})\subseteq(0,\infty)
be such that
a_{n}\rightarrow 0
.
Let
(y,s_{n})=(f(\sigma-)+\epsilon,\sigma-a_{n})

and note that
(y,s_{n})\rightarrow(y,\sigma)
where
y=f(\sigma-)+\epsilon
.
By pervasiveness of
\mathcal{A}
let
g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}((y,s_{n}))

so that

g_{n}(s_{n}-)\wedge g_{n}(s_{n}+)\;\leq\;y\;\leq\;g_{n}(s_{n}-)\vee g_{n}(s_{n}+).

(4.4)

Without loss of generality we may take
g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{A}
we may pass to a subsequence and assume that
g_{n}\rightarrow g\in\mathcal{A}
.

Consider if
f\subseteq g_{n}
for infinitely many
n
.
For such
n
, noting from (
4.3
) that
f
jumps rightwards at
\sigma
, we have
g_{n}(\sigma-)\leq f(\sigma-)
.
From (
4.4
) we have
g_{n}(s_{n}-)\vee g_{n}(s_{n}+)\geq y=f(\sigma-)+\epsilon
,
and
s_{n}\pm<\sigma-
with
s_{n}\rightarrow\sigma
,
so Lemma
A.2.3
gives that

g
jumps leftwards at
\sigma
, from right of
f(\sigma-)+\epsilon
to left of
f(\epsilon)
.
This would make
f
and
g\in\mathcal{A}
cross, which is a contradiction.
Hence in fact
f\subseteq g_{n}
for at most finitely many
n
,
so we may pass to a subsequence and assume

f\nsubseteq g_{n}
for all
n
.
If
g_{n}\subseteq f
then we would have
\sigma_{g_{n}}\geq\sigma_{f}
, which is not the case because
\sigma_{g_{n}}\leq s_{n}<\sigma_{f}
.
Hence we also have
g_{n}\nsubseteq f
for all
n
.

Consider if
g_{n}\vartriangleleft f
for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For such
n
, noting that we have
f\nsubseteq g_{n}
and
g_{n}\nsubseteq f
,
by part 2 of Lemma
3.1.5
we have
f\ntriangleleft g_{n}
.
Hence, for such
n
, using that
R_{\sigma-}(f)=(f(\sigma-),\infty]
,
by Lemma
3.1.4
we must have
g_{n}(\sigma-)\leq f(\sigma-)
.
From (
4.4
) we have
y\leq g_{n}(s_{n}-)\vee g_{n}(\sigma_{n}+)
, where
s_{n}<\sigma
with
s_{n}\rightarrow\sigma
.
By Lemma
A.2.3
, taking a limit along a subsequence of such
n
would result in

g(\sigma-)\geq y>f(\sigma-)\geq g(\sigma+)
,
in which case
f
and
g
cross (by jumping over each other in opposite directions at time
\sigma
).
This may not occur.
Hence in fact
g_{n}\vartriangleleft f
for at most finitely many
n
.
By Lemma
3.1.3
for all
n
we have
f\vartriangleleft g_{n}
or
g_{n}\vartriangleleft f
.
We may thus pass to a subsequence and assume that
f\vartriangleleft g_{n}
for all
n
.

We now have
f\vartriangleleft g_{n}
,
f\nsubseteq g_{n}
and
g_{n}\nsubseteq f
.
We will move on to establishing properties of
g_{n}
with
h
.
Here we divide into two cases, based upon whether
h(\sigma-)\leq h(\sigma+)
or
h(\sigma+)<h(\sigma-)
.

•

Firstly, consider if
h(\sigma+)<h(\sigma-)
.

Consider if
h(\sigma-)\leq g_{n}(\sigma-)
for infinitely many
n
.
From (
4.4
) we have
g_{n}(s_{n}-)\wedge g_{n}(s_{n}+)\leq y
and
s_{n}\pm<\sigma-
with
s_{n}\rightarrow\sigma
,
so by Lemma
A.2.3
we obtain that

g
jumps rightwards at
\sigma
, from left of
y
to right of
h(s-)
.
This means that
g\in\mathcal{A}
crosses
h
, which is a contradiction.
Therefore we may pass to a subsequence and assume that
g_{n}(\sigma-)<h(\sigma-)
for all
n
.

We have
\sigma_{g}\leq s_{n}<\sigma
and
\sigma_{h}\leq\sigma
.
If
h\subseteq g_{n}
then, noting that
h
jumps leftwards at
\sigma
, we would have
h_{n}(\sigma-)\leq g_{n}(\sigma-)
,
which is a contradiction.
Hence
h\nsubseteq g_{n}
.
Similarly, if
g_{n}\subseteq h
then
\sigma_{h}\leq\sigma_{g}<\sigma
, so we would have
g_{n}(\sigma-)=h_{n}(\sigma-)
,
which is a contradiction, so
g_{n}\nsubseteq h
.

Consider if
h\vartriangleleft g_{n}
.
We have already seen that
h\nsubseteq g_{n}
and
g_{n}\nsubseteq h
,
so by part 2 of Lemma
3.1.5
we have
g_{n}\ntriangleleft h
.
We have
L_{\sigma-}(h)=[-\infty,h(\sigma-))
so Lemma
3.1.4
gives that
h(\sigma-)\leq g_{n}(\sigma-)
,
which again may not occur.
Hence in fact
g_{n}\vartriangleleft h
.

•

Secondly, consider if
h(\sigma-)\leq h(\sigma+)
.

Our assumption
f\vartriangleleft h
implies that
f(\sigma\bullet)\leq h(s\bullet)
for all
s\bullet\geq\sigma+
,
so from what we have already proved (in the case
t\star\geq\sigma+
)
we may assume without loss of generality that

f(s\bullet)=h(s\bullet)
for all
s\bullet\geq\sigma+
.
Using that
h(\sigma-)\leq h(\sigma+)
we must therefore have
\sigma_{h}<\sigma
,
as otherwise by (
4.3
) we would have
h\subseteq f
.

By left continuity of
h
at
\sigma-
there exists some
\delta>0
such that

h(s\bullet)\geq h(\sigma-)-\epsilon/2
for all
s\bullet\in[\sigma-,(\sigma-\delta)+]
.
From (
4.4
) for all sufficiently large
n
we have

s_{n}\in(\sigma-\delta,s)

and

g_{n}(s_{n}-)\wedge g_{n}(s_{n}+)\;\leq\;y\;<\;h(\sigma-)-\epsilon/2\;\leq\;h(s_{n}\pm).

(4.5)

It follows immediately that, for such
n
,
g_{n}\nsubseteq h
and
h\nsubseteq g_{n}
.
For sufficiently large
n
we also have
s_{n}>\sigma_{h}
,
in which case (
4.5
) gives
g_{n}(s_{n}-)\in L_{s_{n}-}(h)
or
g_{n}(s_{n}+)\in L_{s_{n}+}(h)
.
Hence Lemma
3.1.4
gives
g_{n}\vartriangleleft h
.
We may thus pass to a subsequence and assume that

g_{n}\nsubseteq h
,
h\nsubseteq g_{n}
and
g_{n}\vartriangleleft h
for all
n
.

In both cases we have now shown,
for all
g_{n}
within the subsequence that we have passed into,
that

f\vartriangleleft g_{n}
,
g_{n}\vartriangleleft h
,
and that none of
f,g_{n},h
are
\subseteq
-comparable with each other.
Therefore, any such
g_{n}
has the required properties
and the proof of the present lemma is complete.

\blacksquare

4.2
Weaves of bi-infinite paths

In this section we establish some geometric properties of deterministic weaves that comprise entirely of bi-infinite paths.
One key result is that if
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
is a deterministic weave then set of ramification points of
\mathcal{A}
has zero (two dimensional) Lebesgue measure.
This result will be later extended to all deterministic weaves, in Lemma
4.4.6
.
Whenever we refer to Lebesgue measure in this section, we mean two dimensional Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
.

Lemma 4.2.1

For each
f\in\Pi
, the set
H(f)
has zero Lebesgue measure

Proof.

This lemma is almost self-evident but
in view of the example of Jordan curves with positive Lebesgue measure
we will give a short proof.
Since càdlàg functions have only countably many discontinuities,
the result holds for the part of
H(f)
corresponding to jumps of
f
.
The remaining part of
H(f)
can be shown to have zero Lebesgue measure via Fubini’s theorem.
We leave the details to the reader.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.2.2

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
be a deterministic weave and
h\in\mathcal{A}
.

1.

If
h(t\star)>-\infty
for some
t\star\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
then
there exist a strictly monotone sequence
(f_{n})
with
f_{n}\vartriangleleft f_{n+1}
such that

f_{n}\rightarrow h
.

2.

If
h(t\star)<\infty
for some
t\star\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
then
there exist a strictly monotone sequence
(g_{n})
with
g_{n+1}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
such that

g_{n}\rightarrow h
.

Proof.

We will show only the existence and properties of
(f_{n})
.
The corresponding statements for
(g_{n})
follow by symmetry.
By Lemma
3.1.6

(\mathcal{A},\vartriangleleft)
is totally ordered.
Let
h\in\mathcal{A}
and set
L=\{f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\,;\,f^{\prime}\vartriangleleft h,f^{\prime}\neq h\}
.
As
h(\star)>-\infty
,
by the càdlàg property of
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
there exists some
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that

h
is continuous at
s
and
h(s)>-\infty
.
Taking
g\in\mathcal{A}((h(s)/2,s))
gives
g\in L
, so
L
is non-empty.
By compactness of
\mathcal{A}
the set
\overline{L}
is compact,
which by Lemma
3.3.10

implies that
(\overline{L},\vartriangleleft)
contains a unique maximal element
f
.
By Lemma
3.3.10
we have
f\vartriangleleft h
.

Suppose, in preparation for an argument by contradiction, that
f\neq h
.
Since
f,h
are both bi-infinite there exists
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(t+)<h(t+)
.
By Lemma
4.1.2
and using that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
,
there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}
such that
f\neq g
and
g\neq h
.
This is a contradiction to maximality of
f
in
\overline{L}
.

We thus have
f=h
, which by definition of
L
implies that there exists
(f_{n})\subseteq L
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow h
,
with
f_{n}\neq h
for all
n
.
Without loss of generality we may choose a strictly monotone subsequence, which completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma
4.2.2
fails for general deterministic weaves,
which may contain paths that are isolated points
(from the left, right or both).
For example see the weave
\mathcal{A}
on the right hand side of Figure
2.5.1
.
We will shortly show, as a consequence of Lemma
4.2.4
,
that if a deterministic weave consists entirely of bi-infinite paths
then it does not contain any isolated points.

Lemma 4.2.3

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
be a deterministic weave.
The order topology induced on
\mathcal{A}
by the total order
\vartriangleleft
coincides with its topology as a subspace of
\Pi
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1.6
,
(\mathcal{A},\vartriangleleft)
is totally ordered.
Recall that the order topology on
\mathcal{A}
is generated by the open rays

\displaystyle R_{f}

\displaystyle=\{g\in\mathcal{A}\,;\,g\vartriangleleft f\text{ and }f\neq g\},

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{g\in\mathcal{A}\,;\,f\vartriangleleft g\text{ and }f\neq g\}

where
f\in\mathcal{A}
.
We will show that
R_{f}
is open in the M1 topology on
\mathcal{A}
.
The same result follows for
R^{\prime}_{f}
by a symmetrical argument.
Note that if
f
is the bi-infinite path with constant value at
-\infty
then

R_{f}=\emptyset
and
R^{\prime}_{f}=\mathcal{A}\setminus\{f\}
,
which are automatically open.
Similar considerations apply to if
f
is the bi-infinite path with constant value
\infty
.
We may therefore restrict to
f\in\mathcal{A}
such that
f(t\star)>-\infty
for some
t\star\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

By Lemma
3.1.6

\mathcal{A}
is totally ordered, from which it follows that

\mathcal{A}\setminus R_{g}=\{f\in\mathcal{A}\,;\,g\vartriangleleft f\}
.
By Lemma
3.3.10
this is a closed subset of
\mathcal{A}
in the M1 topology,
thus
R_{g}
is an open subset of
\mathcal{A}
in the M1 topology.
It follows that any subset of
\mathcal{A}
that is open in the order topology is also open in the M1 topology,
and it remains to prove the converse.

It suffices to show that if
B\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is closed in the M1 topology, then it is also closed in the order topology.
Let us write
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M1}}{{\rightarrow}}
for convergence in the M1 topology and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangleleft}}{{\rightarrow}}
for convergence in the order topology.
Let
B\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be closed in the M1 topology i.e. if
f_{n}\in B
and
f_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M1}}{{\rightarrow}}f\in\mathcal{A}
then
f\in B
.
Suppose that
h_{n}\in B
and
h_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangleleft}}{{\rightarrow}}h\in\mathcal{A}
.
By Lemma
4.2.2
there exists
f_{n},g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
f_{n}\vartriangleleft f_{n+1}
,
g_{n+1}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
for all
n
,
and
f_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M1}}{{\rightarrow}}h
,
g_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M1}}{{\rightarrow}}h
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
the set

O_{m}=\{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\,;\,f_{m}\vartriangleleft h^{\prime},h^{\prime}\vartriangleleft g_{m},f_{m}\neq h^{\prime},h^{\prime}\neq g_{m}\}

is an open interval in the order topology,
hence there exists
N_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N_{m}
we have
h_{n}\in O_{N_{m}}
.
Without loss of generality we may assume
N_{m}\rightarrow\infty
as
m\rightarrow\infty
.
We thus have that for all
n\geq N_{m}

f_{m}\vartriangleleft h_{n}\quad\mbox{and}\quad h_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{m}.

(4.6)

Let
h^{\prime}
be any limit point of
(h_{n})
in the M1 topology,
thus
h^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}

Letting
m\rightarrow\infty
in (
4.6
), by Lemma
3.3.10
we obtain

h\vartriangleleft h^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}\vartriangleleft h
.
Since both are bi-infinite, we have
h=h^{\prime}
.
It follows that
h_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M1}}{{\rightarrow}}h
,
which (since
B
is closed in the M1 topology) shows that
h\in B
, and thus completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.2.4

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
be a deterministic weave.
There exists an
order preserving homeomorphism
\phi
between the totally ordered spaces
(\mathcal{A},\vartriangleleft)
and
([0,1],\leq)
.

Proof.

Note that the result of Lemma
4.2.3
is implicit in the statement of the present lemma.
Throughout the proof we will use the result of Lemma
3.1.6
, that
(\mathcal{A},\vartriangleleft)
is totally ordered.
For
f\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
we define

\displaystyle H^{-}(f)

\displaystyle=\{(x,t)\,;\,t\in x<f(t-)\wedge f(t+)\},

\displaystyle H^{+}(f)

\displaystyle=\{(x,t)\,;\,t\in x>f(t-)\vee f(t+)\}.

Note that
\overline{\mathbb{R}}\times\overline{\mathbb{R}}=H^{-}(f)\cup H(f)\cup H^{+}(f)

and that this union is disjoint.
Recall that
d_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}}
is a metric that generates the topology on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}

and recall that for
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
the open
\epsilon
-expansion of
A
is given by

A^{(\epsilon)}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}:\dist_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}}(z,A)<\epsilon\}
,
where
\dist(z,A)=\inf_{a\in A}d_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}}(z,a)
.
Let
\mu
be a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
that is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, with full support.
Let
\phi(f)=\mu(H^{-}(f))
.

It is immediate that
\phi
is non-decreasing and that
\phi(f_{-\infty})=0
,
\phi(f_{\infty})=1

where
f_{\pm\infty}
are the constant paths at
\pm\infty
(it is trivial to check that
f_{\pm\infty}\in\mathcal{A}
).
We next show that
\phi
is continuous.
Assume that
f_{n},f\in\mathcal{A}
and that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
.
From our remarks above Proposition
2.2.1
we have
H(f_{n})\rightarrow H(f)
in the Hausdorff metric induced by
d
.
Hence, for each
\epsilon>0
,
for sufficiently large
n
we have both
H^{-}(f_{n})\subseteq H^{-}(f)^{(\epsilon)}
and
H^{+}(f_{n})\subseteq H^{+}(f)^{(\epsilon)}
.
Thus

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu\big(H^{-}(f_{n})\big)\leq\mu\big(H^{-}(f)^{(\epsilon)}\big)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu\big(H^{+}(f_{n})\big)\leq\mu\big(H^{+}(f)^{(\epsilon)}\big).

Letting
\epsilon\downarrow 0
and using the fact that

\phi_{\mu}(\pi)=\mu\big(H^{-}(\pi)\big)=1-\mu\big(H^{+}(\pi)\big)
by
Lemma
4.2.1
, we see that
\phi_{\mu}(\pi_{n})\rightarrow\phi_{\mu}(\pi)
as

n\rightarrow\infty
.

Our next goal is to show that
\phi_{\mu}
is a bijection.
From what we have already proved,
\phi
is surjective and non-decreasing,
so it suffices to prove that if
f\vartriangleleft g
with
f\neq g
then
\phi(f)<\phi(g)
.
If
f\vartriangleleft g
are not equal then, since both are bi-infinite,
there exists
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(t+)<g(t+)
,
from which it follows by right continuity that the set

O=\{(x,s)\,;\,f(t+)<x<g(t+)\}
has non-empty interior,
and thus positive Lebesgue measure.
Since
O\subseteq H^{-}(g)\setminus H^{-}(f)
and
\mu
is absolutely continuous with Lebesgue measure,
we have
\phi(f)<\phi(g)
, as required.

We have now shown that
\phi
is a continuous bijection from the compact space
\mathcal{A}
to (the Hausdorff topological space)
[0,1]
,
which implies that
\phi
is a homeomorphism.
The fact that
\phi
is non-decreasing and bijective implies that
\phi
is order preserving.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.2.5

The function
(\mathcal{A},z)\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}\{z\text{ is ramified in }\mathcal{A}\}

is measurable from
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
.
Moreover, for any deterministic weave
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}

the set of ramification points of
\mathcal{A}
has zero Lebesgue measure.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\{B\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)\,;\,\exists f\in A\text{ with }B\subseteq\{f\}_{\uparrow}\}

and let
\ram(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
denote the set of ramification points of
\mathcal{A}
.
Note that
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
is non-ramified if and only if
A(z)\in\mathscr{A}
.
It is straightforward to check that
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
is closed,
as a consequence of Lemma
A.2.2
.
From Lemma
A.3.1
the map

(A,z)\mapsto A(z)
from
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
is measurable.
We have

\mathbbm{1}\{z\in\ram(\mathcal{A})\}=\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{A}(z)\in\mathscr{A}\}
,
hence that the map
(\mathcal{A},z)\mapsto\mathbbm{1}\{z\in\ram(A)\}
is measurable.
It follows immediately that
\ram(\mathcal{A})
is a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
,
for any
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.

It remains to show that the measure of
\ram(A)
is zero.
Take
\phi:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow[0,1]
as in the statement of Lemma
4.2.4
,
and let
z\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}\times\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
By Lemma
A.2.2
the set
\mathcal{A}(z)
is closed.
By definition of
\vartriangleleft
(and the fact that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
) we have that
\mathcal{A}(z)
is an interval of the totally ordered space
(\mathcal{A},\vartriangleleft)
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}(z)
is a closed interval of
(\mathcal{A},\vartriangleleft)
and
\phi(\mathcal{A}(z))
is a closed interval of
[0,1]
.

Let
U
be uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
.
Note that
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
is ramified
if and only if the closed interval
\mathcal{A}(z)
is more than just a single point,
which occurs if and only if
\mathbb{P}[U\in\phi(\mathcal{A}(z))]>0
.
Let
\mu
be a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
that is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure.
Let
Z
be a random variable with law
\mu
, independently of
U
.
By Lemma
A.3.1

\mathcal{A}(Z)
is a
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
valued random variable.
Then
\mathbb{P}[U\in\phi(\mathcal{A}(Z))]=\mathbb{P}[\phi^{-1}(U)\in\mathcal{A}(Z)]
is the probability that
the random path
\phi^{-1}(U)
passes through the random point
Z
.
Recalling that
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, by Lemma
4.2.1
this probability is zero.
Thus
Z
is almost surely not ramified,
which implies that the set of ramification points has zero Lebesgue measure.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.2.6

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
be a deterministic weave.
If
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
then
h\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

We will argue by contradiction.
Suppose that
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
and that
h\notin\mathcal{A}
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is closed, this means that
h
is an isolated point of
\mathcal{A}\cup\{h\}
.
Since
h
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
it is straightfoward to check that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{h\}
is a deterministic weave.
Thus, from Lemma
4.2.4
we have that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{h\}
does not contain any isolated points.
This is a contradiction, which completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.2.7

By Lemma
4.2.6
a deterministic weave
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}

always contains a constant path at spatial location
-\infty
, similarly at
+\infty
.
These are the minimal and maximal elements of
(\mathcal{A},\vartriangleleft)
.

Lemma 4.2.8

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
be a deterministic weave.
If
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is dense then
\mathcal{A}=\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}
.

Proof.

It is immediate that
\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
.
Thus
\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}
is compact.
Since
D
is dense, it is easily seen from Lemma
A.2.2

that
\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}
is pervasive, and since
\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
it is also non-crossing.
Hence
\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}
is a deterministic weave.
If
h\in\mathcal{A}
then since
\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}\subseteq\mathcal{A}

we have that
h
does not cross
\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}
.
Hence by Lemma
4.2.6
we have
h\in\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}=\overline{\mathcal{A}(D)}
.

\blacksquare

4.3
Extensions of paths in weaves

Our results on weaves rely fundamentally on the fact that, within a weave,
half-infinite paths may be extended into bi-infinite paths, without inducing crossing.
We also require that such extensions preserve compactness;
this point will be addressed later on in Lemma
4.4.2
.
As one might expect, bi-infinite extensions of paths
are closely related to maximal paths under the partial order
\subseteq
of path extension.
From Lemma
3.1.6
,
if
\mathcal{A}
is a weave then

(\mathcal{A}_{\max},\vartriangleleft)
is totally ordered.
It is tempting to hope that bi-infinite extensions of paths could be constructed via taking a suitable limit
of paths in
\mathcal{A}
,
but in general this is not possible because deterministic weaves are closed sets.
A more delicate operation is required.

Definition 4.3.1

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
We say that a subset
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\max}
is a
Dedekind cut
of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}

if
(i) whenever
f,g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
with
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\in X
we have
f\in X
and
(ii)
X
has no maximal element.

Let us outline the main results within this section.
Recall that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
denotes the set of bi-infinite càdlàg paths that do not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
Dedekind cuts are best known as part of Dedekind’s construction of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
from
\mathbb{Q}
.
A related situation presents itself here,
in which
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
plays the role of
\mathbb{Q}

and
\flow(\mathcal{A})
plays the role of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
.
Specifically:
in Lemma
4.3.2
we show that if
h\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
does not cross
\mathcal{A}

then the paths of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
that lie strictly to the left of
h

are a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
In Theorem
4.3.9
,
which will be proved across several lemmas,
we show that Dedekind cuts of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
are in bijective correspondence with
bi-infinite paths that do not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
Thus, each
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
gives rise to a Dedekind cut,
which in turn gives rise to a bi-infinite path
h
,
extending
f
without crossing
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 4.3.2

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and let
h\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
be a path that does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
Then

X_{h}=\{f\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\,;\,f\vartriangleleft h,\;f\nsubseteq h,\;h\nsubseteq f\}

(4.7)

is a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
If
h,h^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
do not cross
\mathcal{A}
, and are such that
h\nsubseteq h^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}\nsubseteq h
, then

X_{h}\neq X_{h^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let us first check that
X_{h}
is a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
We must check that
X_{h}
satisfies conditions (i) and (ii) of Definition
4.3.1
.
For (i),
suppose that
f\in X
and
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
with
g\vartriangleleft f
.
We have
f\vartriangleleft h
.
Lemma
3.1.6
(which includes that
\vartriangleleft
is transitive) gives that
g\vartriangleleft h
, as required.
For (ii),
let
f\in X_{h}
.
Lemma
4.1.5
implies that there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
, such that

f\nsubseteq g
,
g\nsubseteq h
,
g\nsubseteq h
,
h\nsubseteq g
, with
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
.
As
f,g,h\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
this implies that
f,g,h
are all distinct.
Thus
f
is not a maximal element of
X
, as required.

It remains to check that
X_{h}\neq X_{h^{\prime}}
whenever
h,h^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}

do not cross
\mathcal{A}
and satisfy
h\nsubseteq h^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}\nsubseteq h
.
Noting that both
h
and
h^{\prime}
do not cross
\mathcal{A}
,
by Lemma
4.1.3

h
and
h^{\prime}
also do not cross each other.
Lemma
3.1.3
gives that
h\vartriangleleft h^{\prime}
or
h^{\prime}\vartriangleleft h
.
Without loss of generality suppose that
h\vartriangleleft h^{\prime}
.
Then by Lemma
4.1.5
there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}

such that
g\nsubseteq h
,
h\nsubseteq g
,
g\nsubseteq h^{\prime}
,
h^{\prime}\nsubseteq g
with
h\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h^{\prime}
.
It follows that
g\in X_{h}
and
g\notin X_{h^{\prime}}
, as required.

\blacksquare

We now consider the inverse map to (
4.7
),
in that we seek to reconstruct a bi-infinite path from its corresponding Dedekind cut.
This part is rather technical and will involve the topology introduced in Section
2.1
on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
which we invite the reader to recall at this point.
In particular
recall from Lemma
2.1.1
that

t_{n}\star_{n}\rightarrow t+

if and if and only if
t_{n}\rightarrow t
and
t_{n}\star_{n}\geq t+
for all sufficiently large
n
;
similarly

t_{n}\star_{n}\rightarrow t-

if and if and only if
t_{n}\rightarrow t
and
t_{n}\star_{n}\leq t-
for all sufficiently large
n
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a weave and
X
is a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
then we set

\mathscr{L}(X)=\bigcup_{f\in X}L(f)

(4.8)

where
L(f)
is defined in (
3.1
).
Note that
\mathscr{L}(X)
is a subset of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
which we equip with the product topology.
Recall also that
L_{t\star}(f)=\{x\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}\,;\,(x,t\star)\in\mathscr{L}(X)\}

which, according to (
3.1
) is either empty or equal to
[-\infty,f(t\star))
.
Roughly, our strategy is to show that
the right-hand boundary of
\mathscr{L}(X)
is the graph (in space-time
\overline{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
) of a càdlàg path.
With this in mind, given a Dedekind cut
X
of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
let
\mathscr{P}_{X}:\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
be given by

\mathscr{P}_{X}(t\star)=\sup\left\{x\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}\,;\,(x,t\star)\in\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}\right\}.

(4.9)

Taking the closure of
\mathscr{L}(X)
in (
4.9
) is crucial,
because being a càdlàg path corresponds to being a continuous function on
\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
and being a continuous function corresponds to having a closed graph.

Lemma 4.3.3

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and let
X
be a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
If
(y,t\star)\in\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
and
x\leq y
then
(x,t\star)\in\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
.

Proof.

Let
(y,t\star)\in\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
and
x<y

By (
4.8
) there exists
f_{n}\in X
and
(y_{n},t_{n}\star_{n})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}

such that
(y_{n},t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow y
and
y_{n}\in L_{t_{n}\star_{n}}(f_{n})
.
Thus
x\leq y_{n}
for all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For such
n
we have
x_{n}\in L_{t_{n}\star_{n}}(f_{n})
,
which implies
(x_{n},t_{n}\star_{n})\in\mathscr{L}(X)
,
hence
(x,t\star)\in\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.3.4

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and let
X
be a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
The following hold:

1.

Let
f\in X
and
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
. Then
f\vartriangleleft g
.

2.

Suppose that
(x,t\star)\in(\overline{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}})\setminus\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
.
Then for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}

and
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
such that
|t-t^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon
and
g\in\mathcal{A}((x,t^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

For the first part, take
f\in X
and
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
.
Note that
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
so
f
and
g
may not cross,
which by Lemma
3.1.3
implies that
f\vartriangleleft g
or
g\vartriangleleft f
.
If
f\subseteq g
or
g\subseteq f
then by maximality
f=g
, in which case
f\vartriangleleft g
.
Alternatively, if both
f\nsubseteq g
and
g\nsubseteq f
then
g\vartriangleleft f
would imply
g\in X
,
because
X
is a Dedekind cut; so we must have
f\vartriangleleft g
.
Thus, in all cases we have
f\vartriangleleft g
.

Let us now consider the second claim.
First consider the case
t\star=t+
.
Let
\epsilon>0
.
As
(x,t+)\notin\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that

\big([x-\epsilon_{0},x+\epsilon_{0}]\times[t+,(t+\epsilon_{0})+]\big)\cap\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}=\emptyset.

(4.10)

Without loss of generality, assume
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
.
By pervasiveness of
\mathcal{A}
there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}((x,t^{\prime}))
where
t^{\prime}=t+\epsilon/2
.
Without loss of generality we may take
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
It is clear that
|t-t^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon
.

Consider if
g\in X
.
Note that we have
g(t^{\prime}+)\vee g(t^{\prime}-)\geq x
.
If
g(t^{\prime}+)\geq x
then
[-\infty,x)\subseteq L_{g}(t^{\prime}+)

which would imply
(x,t^{\prime}+)\in\overline{L(g)}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
, contradicting (
4.10
), so this may not happen.
The remaining case is that
g(t^{\prime}+)<x\leq g(t^{\prime}-)
,
in which case
[-\infty,x)\subseteq L_{t^{\prime}-}(g)
, implying that
(x,t^{\prime})\in\overline{L(g)}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
,
contradicting (
4.10
),
so this may not happen either.
We conclude that
g\notin X
.
This completes the proof of the case
t\star=t+
.
The case
t=t-
is similar, using in place of (
4.10
)
that for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
we have

[x-\epsilon_{0},x+\epsilon_{0}]\times[(t-\epsilon_{0})-,t-]\cap\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}=\emptyset
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.3.5

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and let
X
be a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
Then
\mathscr{P}_{X}
is a bi-infinite càdlàg path.

Proof.

Let us write
h=\mathscr{P}_{X}
for the duration of this proof.
We must show that
h
is a continuous map from
\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
.
By the closed graph theorem,
the function
h:\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
is continuous if and only if its graph

\mathscr{H}=\{(h(t\star),t\star)\,;\,t\star\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}\}

is a closed subset of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Let
t_{n}\star_{n}\rightarrow t\star
in
\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
By compactness of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
the sequence
(h(t_{n}\star_{n}),t_{n}\star_{n})
is relatively compact.
Let
(x,t\star)
be a limit point of this sequence,
and (with slight abuse of notation) let us pass to a subsequence such that
h(t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow x
.
To establish the present lemma we must show that
x=h(t\star)
.

By (
4.9
),
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a sequence
(x_{n,m},t_{n,m}\star_{n,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(X)

such that

(x_{n,m},t_{n,m}\star_{n,m})\rightarrow(h(t_{n}\star_{n}),t_{n}\star_{n})

as
m\rightarrow\infty
.
By a diagonal argument there exists a strictly increasing function
m:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that

(x_{n,m(n)},t_{n,m(n)}\star_{n,m(n)})\rightarrow(x,t\star)
.
Hence
(x,t\star)\in\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
,
which implies that
x\leq h(t\star)
.
If
h(t\star)=-\infty
then we now have
x=h(t\star)
,
so in what follows we may assume that

-\infty<h(t\star)
.

We will now argue by contradiction:
suppose that
x<h(t\star)
.
Let
\epsilon>0
be such that
x+3\epsilon\leq h(t\star)
,
and note that
x+\epsilon<h(t\star)-\epsilon
.
We consider the cases
t\star=t+
and
t\star=t-
in turn.

Suppose, first, that
t\star=t+
. Let us briefly outline the strategy.
We will construct a sequence of

f_{n}\in X
that come close to the space-time point
(h(t+),t+)
,
and a sequence of
g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
that come close to
(h(t_{n}\star_{n}),t_{n}\star_{n})\approx(x,t+)
.
Note that
x<h(t+)
, whilst
Lemma
4.3.4
gives
f_{j}\vartriangleleft g_{k}
for all
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.
This combination causes
(f_{n})
and
(g_{n})
to become tangled up in each other,
so much so that their limit points
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
will cross,
by jumping over each other in opposite directions at time
t
,
resulting in a contradiction.
We now proceed with the proof.

By Lemma
2.1.1
, the fact that
t_{n}\star_{n}\rightarrow t+
implies that for sufficiently large
n
we must have
t_{n}\star_{n}\geq t+
.
As
h(t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow x<h(t+)
, in fact for sufficiently large
n
we have
t_{n}\star_{n}>t+
,
and also
h(t_{n}\star_{n})\leq x+\epsilon
.
Without loss of generality we pass to a subsequence and assume that both these properties hold for all
n
.

By (
4.9
) there exists
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(X)
such that

(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\rightarrow(h(t+),t+)
.
The fact that
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\rightarrow t+
implies that for sufficiently large
n
we must have
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\geq t+
,
and for sufficiently large
n
we also have
y_{n}\geq h(t\star)-\epsilon
.
so without loss of generality we pass to a subsequence and assume that both these properties hold for all
n
.

Consider if

h(t+)=\sup\{x\,;\,(x,t+)\in\mathscr{L}(X)\}.

(4.11)

In this case there exists
f\in X
such that
L_{t+}(f)=[-\infty,f(t+))
and
0<h(t+)-f(t+)\leq\epsilon
,
so
f(t+)\geq x+2\epsilon
.
Right continuity (i.e. forwards in time) of
f
thus implies
h(t_{n}\star_{n})\geq x+\epsilon
for all sufficiently large
n
,
which contradicts the fact that
h(t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow x
.
So this case may not occur.

Therefore,
h(t+)\neq\sup\{x\,;\,(x,t+)\in\mathscr{L}(X)\}
,
which implies that for sufficiently large
n
we have
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\neq t+

(because
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\rightarrow(h(t+),t+)
and
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in\mathscr{L}(X)
).
We have already seen that
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\geq t+
, so
without loss of generality we pass to a subsequence and assume that
s_{n}\bullet_{n}>t+
for all
n
.

As
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in\mathscr{L}(X)
there exists
f_{n}\in X
such that
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in L(f_{n})
.
Hence
f_{n}(s_{n}\bullet_{n})\geq y_{n}\geq h(t\star)-\epsilon
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{A}
,
without loss of generality we pass to a subsequence and assume that
f_{n}\rightarrow f\in\mathcal{A}
.

Let
x_{n}=h(t_{n}\star_{n})+2^{-n}
.
As
h(t_{n}\star_{n})\rightarrow x
, without loss of generality we pass to a subsequence and assume that

x_{n}\leq x+\epsilon
for all
n
.
Lemma
4.3.3
gives that
(x_{n},t_{n}\star_{n})\notin\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}
.
Thus, by the second part of Lemma
4.3.4
,
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
t_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X

such that
|t_{n}-t_{n}^{\prime}|\leq 2^{-n}
,
t^{\prime}_{n}\star_{n}>t+
and
g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}((x_{n},t^{\prime}_{n}))
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{A}
,
without loss of generality we pass to a subsequence and assume that
g_{n}\rightarrow g\in\mathcal{A}
.
By the first part of Lemma
4.3.4
we have
f_{i}\vartriangleleft g_{j}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
.

We have that
t^{\prime}_{n}\star_{n}
and
s_{n}\bullet_{n}
are both strictly greater than
t+
,
and both tend to
t+
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Consequently,
passing to further subsequences,
there exists a strictly increasing function
n\mapsto n^{\prime}
such that

s_{n+1}\bullet_{n+1}<t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\star_{n^{\prime}}<s_{n}\bullet_{n}

(4.12)

for all
n
.

We now examine the sequence
(g_{n})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
We will show that

\displaystyle g_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-)\vee g_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}+)

\displaystyle\leq x_{n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq x+\epsilon,

(4.13)

\displaystyle g_{n^{\prime}}(s_{n}\bullet_{n})

\displaystyle\geq y_{n}

\displaystyle\geq h(t\star)-\epsilon.

(4.14)

Equation (
4.13
) follows because
g_{n^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{A}((x_{n^{\prime}},t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}))
.
To see equation (
4.14
): we have that
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n^{\prime}}

and that
[-\infty,y_{n})\subseteq L_{s_{n}\bullet_{n}}(f_{n})
.
Lemma
3.1.2
implies
L(f_{n})\cap R(g_{n^{\prime}})=\emptyset
,
and
\sigma_{g_{n^{\prime}}}<s_{n}\bullet_{n}
so we must have
g_{n^{\prime}}(s_{n}\bullet_{n})\geq y_{n}
.

From Lemma
A.2.2
,
combined with (
4.12
), (
4.13
) and (
4.14
)
we obtain that the limit
g
makes a rightwards jump at time
t
,
from below
x+\epsilon
at time
t-
to above
h(t\star)-\epsilon
at time
t+
.

We now turn our attention to
(f_{n})
, in similar style.
Here, we show that

\displaystyle f_{n+1}(s_{n+1}\bullet_{n+1})

\displaystyle\geq y_{n+1}

\displaystyle\geq h(t\star)-\epsilon

(4.15)

\displaystyle f_{n+1}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-)\wedge f_{n+1}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}+)

\displaystyle\leq x_{n}

\displaystyle\leq x+\epsilon

(4.16)

Equation (
4.15
) follows from the fact that
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in L(f_{n})
.
To see equation (
4.16
): we have that
f_{n+1}\vartriangleleft g_{n^{\prime}}
.
If
g_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}+)\leq g_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-)
then we have

f_{n+1}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}+)\leq g_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}+)\leq x_{n}
.
Alternatively, if
g_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-)<g_{n^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}+)
then we have

[-\infty,x_{n})\subseteq R_{t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-}(g_{n^{\prime}})
,
and Lemma
3.1.2
gives
L(f_{n+1})\cap R(g_{n^{\prime}})=\emptyset
,
which implies
f_{n+1}(t^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-)\leq x_{n}
.
In both cases we have (
4.16
).

From Lemma
A.2.2

combined with (
4.12
), (
4.15
) and (
4.16
)
we obtain that the limit
f
makes a leftwards jump at time
t
,
from above
h(t\star)-\epsilon
at time
t-
to below
x+\epsilon
at time
t+
.
Thus
f
and
g
cross (by jumping in opposite directions over each other at time
t
).
As both
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
, this is a contradiction.
This completes the proof of the case
t\star=t+
.

It remains to consider the case
t\star=t-
.
The argument is essentially the same,
except that Lemma
2.1.1
requires that we now
approach
t-
from the left (i.e. from backwards in time) rather than
t+
from the right.
In outline: construct a sequence of

f_{n}\in X
that come close to the space-time point
(h(t-),t-)
,
and a sequence of
g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
that come close to
(h(t_{n}\star_{n}),t_{n}\star_{n})\approx(x,t-)
.
Note that
x<h(t-)
, whilst
Lemma
4.3.4
gives
f_{j}\vartriangleleft g_{k}
for all
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
.
This combination causes
(f_{n})
and
(g_{n})
to become entangled with each other,
so that once again their limit points
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
will cross – resulting in a contradiction.

There is one point at which a difference worthy of comment emerges.
This concerns (
4.11
).
If
h(t-)=\sup\{x\,;\,(x,t-)\in\mathscr{L}(X)\}
then
there exists
f\in X
such that
L_{t-}(f)=[-\infty,f(t-))
and
0<h(t-)-f(t-)\leq\epsilon
.

•

If
\sigma_{f}<t
then a similar argument to that in the same paragraph as (
4.11
) applies,
using left continuity of
f
instead of right continuity;
this reaches a contradiction.

•

If
\sigma_{f}=t
then we require a new step within the argument,
one that features only here because of the ‘extra’ behaviour of
L_{t\star}(f)
when
t\star=\sigma_{f}-
, see (
3.1
).
In particular, for
t=\sigma_{f}
the fact that
L_{t-}(f)=[-\infty,f(t-))
implies that
f(t+)<f(t-)
.
We then proceed as before to construct
g\in\mathcal{A}
such that
g(t-)\leq x+\epsilon
and
g(t+)\geq h(t-)-\epsilon
.
Thus
f
and
g
cross, reaching a contradiction.

If
h(t-)\neq\{x\,;\,(x,t-)\in\mathscr{L}(X)\}

then we can (and moreover can only) approximate
(h(t-),t-)\in\overline{\mathscr{L}(X)}

using space-time points in
\mathscr{L}(X)
with times strictly less than
t-
.
In this case we may proceed as before.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.3.6

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and let
X
be a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
Then
\mathscr{P}_{X}
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
Moreover, if
f\in X
then
f\vartriangleleft\mathscr{P}_{X}
,
and if
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
then
\mathscr{P}_{X}\vartriangleleft g
.

Proof.

Let us write
h=\mathscr{P}_{X}
for the duration of this proof.
From Lemma
4.3.5
we have
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.
Note that
h
crosses
\mathcal{A}
if and only if
h
crosses
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
We will show, in turn, that
(a)
f\in X\Rightarrow f\vartriangleleft h

and
(b)
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X\Rightarrow h\vartriangleleft g
.
With this in hand it follows from Lemma
3.1.3
that
h
does not cross
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
,
thus
h
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.

We begin with (a).
Let
f\in X
and
