t\star\geq\sigma_{f}-
.
If
L_{t\star}(f)=[-\infty,f(t\star))
then

(f(t\star),t\star)\in\mathscr{L}(X)
and hence
f(t\star)\leq h(t\star)
,
which implies
L_{t\star}(f)\cap R_{t\star}(h)=\emptyset
.
Alternatively, if
L_{t\star}(f)=\emptyset
then it is immediate that
L_{t\star}(f)\cap R_{t\star}(h)=\emptyset
.
Hence
L(f)\cap R(g)=\emptyset
, which by Lemma
3.1.4
implies that
f\vartriangleleft h
.

We now move on to (b).
Let
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
.
We will argue by contradiction.
Suppose that
h\ntriangleleft g
.
Then by Lemma
3.1.2
we have
L(h)\cap R(g)\neq\emptyset
.
In particular,
for some
t\star\geq\sigma_{g}-
we have

L_{t\star}(h)=[-\infty,h(t\star))
and

R_{t\star}(g)=(g(t\star),\infty]

with
g(t\star)<h(t\star)
.

Consider first if
t\star\geq\sigma_{g}+
.
Then, using the càdlàg property of
g
and
h

there exists
\epsilon>0
an interval
[a+,b-]
with
a<b

such that

g(s\bullet)+\epsilon\leq h(\sigma\bullet)

(4.17)

for all
s\bullet\in[a-,b+]

(to see this: if
\star=+
take
a=t
, if
\star=-
take
b=t
).
Let us briefly note our strategy here: we will use (
4.17
) to
show that
g
lies to the left of some path in
X
.
Fix some
s\bullet\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
with
a+<s\bullet<b-
.
By (
4.9
) there exists
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in\mathscr{L}(X)

such that
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\rightarrow(h(s\bullet),s\bullet)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
For all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

h(s\bullet)-\epsilon/2\leq y_{n}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad a-\leq s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq b+.

(4.18)

By Lemma
4.3.5
we have
h(s_{n}\bullet_{n})\rightarrow h(s\bullet)
,
so for all sufficiently large
n
we also have

|h(s\bullet)-h(s_{n}\bullet_{n})|\leq\epsilon/2.

(4.19)

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough that (
4.18
) and (
4.19
) both hold.
By (
4.8
) there exists
f_{n}\in X
such that

(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in L_{s_{n}\bullet_{n}}(f)
,
which means that
y_{n}<f(s_{n}\bullet_{n})
.
Combining this inequality with (
4.17
), (
4.18
) and (
4.19
) we obtain that

g(s_{n}\bullet_{n})\;\leq\;h(s_{n}\bullet_{n})-\epsilon\;\leq\;h(s\bullet)-\epsilon/2\;\leq\;y_{n}\;<\;f_{n}(s_{n}\bullet_{n}).

Hence
g(s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in L_{s_{n}\bullet_{n}}(f)
,
which by Lemma
3.1.4
means that
g\vartriangleleft f_{n}
.
As
X
is a Dedekind cut and
f_{n}\in X
, we thus have
g\in X
, which is a contradiction.

It remains to consider the case
t\star=\sigma_{g}-
.
In this case by (
3.1
) we have
R_{\sigma-}(g)=(g(\sigma-),\infty]
and
g(\sigma-)<g(\sigma+)
.
We have also that
g(\sigma-)<h(\sigma-)
.
By (
4.9
) there exists
(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\in\mathscr{L}(X)
such that

(y_{n},s_{n}\bullet_{n})\rightarrow(h(\sigma-),\sigma-)
,
and
f_{n}\in X
such that
y_{n}\in L_{s_{n}\bullet_{n}}(f)=[-\infty,f_{n}(s_{n}\bullet_{n}))
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{A}
we may pass to a subsequence and assume that
f_{n}\rightarrow f\in\mathcal{A}
.
Without loss of generality we may assume that
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq s-
,
so
\sigma_{f_{n}}\leq\sigma_{g}
.

Suppose that
f_{n}(\sigma-)\geq g(\sigma-)
: then
f_{n}(\sigma-)\in R_{\sigma-}(g)
which,
by Lemma
3.1.4
would give
g\vartriangleleft f_{n}
,
and as
X
is a Dedekind cut this would give
g\in X
, which is false.
Hence in fact
f_{n}(\sigma-)<g(\sigma-)
.
We now have
s_{n}\bullet_{n}\leq\sigma-
with
\sigma_{n}\bullet_{n}\rightarrow\sigma-
, along with
f_{n}(s_{n}\bullet_{n})\geq y_{n}

and
f_{n}(\sigma-)\leq g(\sigma-)
.
By Lemma
A.2.3
, this implies that
f
jumps leftwards at
\sigma
,
from right of
h(\sigma-)
to left of
g(\sigma-)
.
This implies that
g
and
f
cross, by jumping over each other in opposite directions as
\sigma
,
which is a contradiction as both
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.3.7

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and suppose that
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
Define
X=X_{h}
according to (
4.7
).
Then
\mathscr{P}_{X}=h
.

Proof.

Let us write
h^{\prime}=\mathscr{P}_{X}
for the duration of this proof.
We must show that
h=h^{\prime}
.
We will argue by contradiction.
Noting that
h
is bi-infinite,
if
h\neq h^{\prime}
then there exist
t+\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
such that
h(t+)\neq h^{\prime}(t+)
.
Without loss of generality (or consider space reflected about the origin)
we may assume that
h(t+)<h^{\prime}(t+)
.
Thus
h\vartriangleleft h^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
4.1.2
,
again using that
h
and
h^{\prime}
are bi-infinite,
there exists
g\in\mathcal{A}

such that
h\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h^{\prime}
with
g\nsubseteq h
and
g\nsubseteq h^{\prime}
.
Without loss of generality we may take
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
Hence either
g\in X
or
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
.
We consider these two cases separately.

If
g\in X
then,
recalling that

X=\{f\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\,;\,f\vartriangleleft h\text{ and }f\nsubseteq h\}
,
we have
g\vartriangleleft h
.
From Lemma
3.1.3
, noting that
h
is bi-infinite,
we thus obtain
g\subseteq h
, which is a contradiction.
If
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X
then
by Lemma
4.3.6
we have
h^{\prime}\vartriangleleft g
.
From Lemma
3.1.3
, noting that
h^{\prime}
is bi-infinite,
we thus obtain
g\subseteq h^{\prime}
, which is a contradiction.
Having reached a contradiction in both cases,
we conclude that in fact
h=h^{\prime}
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.3.8

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and
let
X
be a Dedekind cut of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.
There exists
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
such that
X=X_{h}
,
where
X_{h}
is given by (
4.7
).

Proof.

Let us write
h=\mathscr{P}_{X}
for the duration of this proof.
By Lemma
4.3.5
we have
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.
Define
X_{h}
as in (
4.7
),
that is

X_{h}=\{f\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\,;\,f\leq h\text{ and }f\nsubseteq h\}
.
Note that since
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
we may discard the condition
h\nsubseteq f
,
because if
h\subseteq f
then
f=h
and thus also
f\subseteq h
.
We must show that
X=X_{h}
.

Let
f\in X
.
Lemma
4.3.6
gives that
f\vartriangleleft h
.
Moreover Lemma
4.3.6
gives that

g\vartriangleleft h
for all
g\in X
.
Thus, if
f\subseteq h
then we would also have
g\vartriangleleft f
for all
g\in X
,
which would make
f
a maximal element of
X
;
this a contradiction as
X
is a Dedekind cut.
Hence
f\nsubseteq h
.
We thus have
X\subseteq X_{h}
.
We now move on to the reverse inclusion.

Let
f\in X_{h}
,
so we have
f\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}
,
f\vartriangleleft h
and
f\nsubseteq h
.
If
f\notin X
then Lemma
4.3.6
gives that
h\vartriangleleft f
,
from which part 2 of Lemma
3.1.5
implies that
f\subseteq h
or
h\subseteq f
;
this is a contradiction.
Hence
X_{h}\subseteq X
, so
X=X_{h}
as required.

\blacksquare

Theorem 4.3.9

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Let
\mathscr{X}
denote the set of Dedekind cuts of
\mathcal{A}_{\max}
.

1.

The map
h\mapsto X_{h}
given by (
4.7
)
is a bijection between
\flow(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathscr{X}
.
The inverse map
X\mapsto\mathscr{P}_{X}
is given by (
4.9
).

2.

For any
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
that does not cross
\mathcal{A}
,
there exists
h\in\flow(\mathcal{A})
such that
f\subseteq h
.

Proof.

Recall from (
2.13
) that by definition
\flow(\mathcal{A})=\{h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}\,;\,h\text{ does not cross }\mathcal{A}\}
.
Lemma
4.3.2
gives that the range of the map
h\mapsto X_{h}
(with domain
\flow(\mathcal{A})
) is within
\mathscr{X}

and that this map is injective.
Lemma
4.3.8
gives that
h\mapsto X_{h}
has range
\mathscr{X}
.
Lemmas
4.3.5
and
4.3.6
ensure that the range of the map
X\mapsto\mathscr{P}_{X}

(with domain
\mathscr{X}
)
is within
\flow(\mathcal{A})
,
so Lemma
4.3.7
gives that
h\mapsto X_{h}
and
X\mapsto\mathscr{P}_{X}
are inverses of each other,
between
\flow(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathscr{X}
.
This establishes the first claim of the present theorem.

To see the second claim, let
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
and suppose that
f
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
By Lemma
4.3.2
we have
X_{f}\in\mathscr{X}
,
from which part 1 of the present theorem gives that
h=\mathscr{P}_{X_{f}}\in\flow(\mathcal{A})

does not cross
\mathcal{A}
.
It remains to show that
f\subseteq h
.
We will argue by contradiction.

Suppose that
f\nsubseteq h
,
which as
h\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
implies that
h\nsubseteq f
.
We have that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{f\}
is non-crossing and that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{h\}
is non-crossing.
It follows by Lemma
4.1.3
that
\mathcal{A}\cup\{f,h\}
is non-crossing,
so in particular
f
does not cross
h
.
By Lemma
3.1.3
we have

f\vartriangleleft h
or
h\vartriangleleft f
.
We treat these two cases in turn.

Consider, first, if
f\vartriangleleft h
.
Then Lemma
4.1.5
gives
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}

such that
f\vartriangleleft g
,
g\vartriangleleft h
, with
f,g,h
all incomparable under
\subseteq
.
Hence
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}\setminus X_{f}
, which by Lemma
4.3.6
gives
h\vartriangleleft g
.
By Lemma
3.1.6
we thus have
g\subseteq h
or
h\subseteq g
, which is a contradiction.

The argument when
h\vartriangleleft f
is similar.
Now Lemma
4.1.5
gives
g\in\mathcal{A}_{\max}

such that
h\vartriangleleft g
,
g\vartriangleleft f
with
f,g,h
all incomparable under
\subseteq
.
Hence
g\in X_{f}
,
which by Lemma
4.3.6
gives
g\vartriangleleft h
,
and Lemma
3.1.6
again arrives at a contradiction.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

4.4
The flow map

In this section we establish further properties of the
\flow
operation defined in (
2.13
),
including Lemma
4.4.3
which shows that
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\flow(\mathcal{A})
is continuous on
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
Another key result, contained within Lemma
4.4.1
,
is that the path extension of Theorem
4.3.9
preserves compactness.
Here, for the first time,
we see interaction between all the main concepts of the present article:
relative compactness in
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
,
path extension,
and the pervasiveness and non-crossing properties of weaves.

Lemma 4.4.1

Let
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
be a relatively compact subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
,
where each
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{A}
is a deterministic weave.
Suppose that any limit point of
\mathscr{A}
is non-crossing.
Then
\{\flow(\mathcal{A})\,;\,\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{A}\}
is a relatively compact subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\updownarrow})
.

Proof.

As
\Pi^{\updownarrow}
is a closed subset of
\Pi^{\uparrow}
,
also
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\updownarrow})
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
.
It therefore suffices to consider relative compactness in
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
.
By Lemma
A.1.1
the set

\{\flow(\mathcal{A})\,;\,\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{A}\}

is a relatively compact subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})

if and only if

\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{A}}\flow(\mathcal{A})

is a relatively compact subset of
\Pi^{\uparrow}
.
We will argue by contradiction.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is not relatively compact.
Then, by Proposition
A.2.1
there exists

T,\kappa>0
and sequences
(\delta_{n})\subseteq(0,1)
,
(f_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
such that

\delta_{n}\searrow 0
and
w_{T,\delta_{n}}(f_{n})\geq\kappa
.
For convenience, recall from (
A.2
) that

\displaystyle w_{T,\delta}(f)=\sup\big\{d_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}\big(f(t_{2}\star_{2}),[f(t_{1}\star_{1}),f(t_{3}\star_{3})]\big)\,;\,

\displaystyle t_{1}\star_{1},t_{2}\star_{2},t_{3}\star_{3}\in I_{\mathfrak{s}}(f),

\displaystyle-T<t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}<T,\ t_{3}-t_{1}<\delta\big\}.

So, we have
t^{n}_{i}\star^{n}_{i}\in I_{\mathfrak{s}}(f_{n})
such that
-T<t_{1}^{n}<t_{2}^{n}<t_{3}^{n}<T
and
t^{n}_{3}-t^{n}_{1}<\delta
,
with

d_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}\left(f_{n}(t^{n}_{2}\star^{n}_{2}),\left[f_{n}(t^{n}_{1}\star^{n}_{1}),f(t^{n}_{3}\star^{n}_{3})\right]\right)\geq\kappa.

(4.20)

By the càdlàg property of the bi-infinite path
f_{n}
, we may assume without loss of generality (reducing
\kappa>0
if necessary) that
f_{n}
is continuous at
t^{n}_{i}\star^{n}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
.
Using that
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
is compact, without loss of generality we may pass to a subsequence and assume additionally that

f_{n}(t^{n}_{i})\rightarrow y_{i}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
.
Using that
[-T,T]
is compact, we may pass to a further subsequence and assume additionally that

t^{n}_{i}\rightarrow t_{i}
, with
t_{i}\in[-T,T]
.
Since
0\leq t^{n}_{3}-t^{n}_{1}<\delta_{n}
in fact
t_{1}=t_{2}=t_{3}=t
.
To summarise, we thus have

t^{n}_{1}<t^{n}_{2}<t^{n}_{3}\text{ for all $n$ and }t^{n}_{i}\rightarrow t\in[-T,T]\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

(4.21)

Without loss of generality (or consider the same setup with space reflected about the origin) we pass to a further subsequence and assume additionally that

f_{n}(t^{n}_{1})\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})
,
which by (
4.20
) implies that

f_{n}(t^{n}_{i})+\kappa\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})\quad\text{ for }i=1,3.

(4.22)

Note that
f_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, for some
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{A}
,
and let us write
\mathcal{A}_{n}
for such
\mathcal{A}
.
By pervasiveness of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
there exist

g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}\left((t^{n}_{1},f_{n}(t^{n}_{1})+\tfrac{\kappa}{3})\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad h_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}\left((t^{n}_{2},f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})-\tfrac{\kappa}{3})\right).

(4.23)

Since
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is a weave,
g_{n}
and
h_{n}
do not cross each other.
By Lemma
A.1.1
, relative compactness of
\mathscr{A}
gives that

\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{A}}\mathcal{A}
is a relatively compact subset of
\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.
Hence we may pass to a further subsequence and assume additionally that

g_{n}\rightarrow g\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}
and
h_{n}\rightarrow h\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}
.
The sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{n})
is a subset of
\mathscr{A}
and therefore is relatively compact,
so we may pass to a subsequence and assume that
\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
.
Hence
g,h\in\mathcal{A}
.
The set
\mathcal{A}
is a limit point of
\mathscr{A}
,
therefore (as a hypothesis of the present lemma)
\mathcal{A}
is non-crossing.
Hence
g
and
h
may not cross.

Let us briefly comment on the strategy for the remaining part of the proof:
we will establish a contradiction through showing that
g
and
h
cross at time
t
,
at which time they will jump past each other in opposite directions.
By Lemma
3.1.3
we have that
g_{n},h_{n}
are comparable under
\vartriangleleft
to
f_{n}
.
Since
f_{n}
is continuous at
t^{n}_{i}
,
by (
4.23
) and Lemma
3.1.2
we have

f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
and
h_{n}\vartriangleleft f_{n}
.
Hence

f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})\leq g_{n}(t^{n}_{2}\pm)
and
h_{n}(t^{n}_{3}\pm)\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{3})
.
By definition of
g_{n},h_{n}
we have

g_{n}(t^{n}_{1}-)\wedge g_{n}(t^{n}_{1}+)\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{1})+\frac{\kappa}{3}
and
f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})-\frac{\kappa}{3}\leq h_{n}(t^{n}_{2}-)\vee h_{n}(t^{n}_{2}+)
.
Combining these facts with (
4.22
) we thus have
t^{n}_{i}\bullet^{n}_{i}
such that

\displaystyle g_{n}(t^{n}_{1}\bullet^{n}_{1})+\tfrac{2\kappa}{3}

\displaystyle\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{1})+\kappa

\displaystyle\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq g_{n}(t^{n}_{2}\bullet^{n}_{2})

\displaystyle h_{n}(t^{n}_{3}\bullet^{n}_{3})+\kappa

\displaystyle\leq f_{n}(t_{n}^{3})+\kappa

\displaystyle\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq h_{n}(t^{n}_{2}\bullet^{n}_{2})+\tfrac{\kappa}{3}.

Hence,

\displaystyle g_{n}(t^{n}_{1}\bullet^{n}_{1})+\tfrac{\kappa}{6}

\displaystyle\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})-\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle\leq g_{n}(t^{n}_{2}\bullet^{n}_{2})-\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle h_{n}(t^{n}_{3}\bullet^{n}_{3})+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle\leq f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})-\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle\leq h_{n}(t^{n}_{2}\bullet^{n}_{2})-\tfrac{\kappa}{6}.

Using that
[-T-,T+]\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is compact (which follows from Lemma
2.1.1
) we may pass to a subsequence and assume that
for
i=1,2,3
the sequence
(t^{n}_{i}\bullet^{n}_{i})
converges as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
We will now send
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Recalling that
f_{n}(t^{n}_{2})\rightarrow y_{2}
, we thus obtain from Lemma
A.2.2
and (
4.21
) that

\displaystyle g(t-)

\displaystyle<y_{2}-\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle<g(t+)

\displaystyle h(t+)

\displaystyle<y_{2}-\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle<h(t-),

which implies that
g
and
h
cross. This is a contradiction since
g,h\in\mathcal{A}
.

\blacksquare

The M1 topology and particular form of
w_{T,\delta}
is crucial to the above argument.
We do not know of an analogous result for the J1 topology.

Lemma 4.4.2

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Then
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is a deterministic weave and
\flow(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.

Proof.

Note that equation (
2.13
) gives that
\flow(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.
Let us first establish that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is compact.
By Lemma
4.4.1
(taking
\mathscr{A}=\{\mathcal{A}\}
)
we have that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is relatively compact.
We will show that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is closed (and thus compact).
Suppose that
g_{n}\rightarrow g
where
g_{n}\in\flow(\mathcal{A})
.
For any
f\in\mathcal{A}
there exists
f^{\prime}\in\flow(\mathcal{A})

such that
f\subseteq f^{\prime}
.
Thus
f^{\prime}
and
g_{n}
do not cross, for all
n
.
Lemma
3.3.6
gives that
f^{\prime}
and
g
do not cross,
which implies that
f
and
g
do not cross.
Thus
f
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
, which implies that
f\in\flow(\mathcal{A})
.

To check that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is a weave,
it remains to show that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is pervasive and non-crossing.
If
g,h\in\flow(\mathcal{A})
then by (
2.13
) we have that

\mathcal{A}\cup\{g\}
is non-crossing and
\mathcal{A}\cup\{h\}
is non-crossing.
By Lemma
4.1.3
we thus have that
\{g,h\}
is non-crossing,
so in fact
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is non-crossing.
If
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{c}
then pervasiveness of
\mathcal{A}
implies that
there exists some
f\in\mathcal{A}
such that
f\in H(f)
.
Theorem
4.3.9
implies that there exists
f^{\prime}\in\flow(\mathcal{A})
with
f\subseteq f^{\prime}
,
hence
z\in H(f^{\prime})
.
Thus
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is pervasive.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.4.3

Let
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}
be deterministic weaves with
\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
.
Then
\flow(\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\flow(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

The set
\mathscr{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\,;\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a relatively compact subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
.
The only limit point of
\mathscr{A}
is
\mathcal{A}
, which is non-crossing.
Thus by Lemma
4.4.1

the set
\{\flow(\mathcal{A}_{n})\,;\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a relatively compact subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
.
Suppose that we have
\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
along some subsequence
and pass to this subsequence, with mild abuse of notation.
To prove the present lemma we must show that
\mathcal{F}=\flow(\mathcal{A})
.

By Lemma
4.4.3
we have that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
is a deterministic weave.
Let us show that
\mathcal{F}
is also a deterministic weave, with
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.
From the previous paragraph we have
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
.
As
\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
and each
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is a subset of the closed set
\Pi^{\updownarrow}
,
we have
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.
If
f,g\in\mathcal{F}
then we have
f_{n},g_{n}\in\flow(\mathcal{A}_{n})
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
We have that
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
do not cross,
so by Lemma
3.3.6

f
and
g
do not cross.
Thus
\mathcal{F}
is non-crossing.
For all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{c}
there exists
f_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
such that
z\in H(f_{n})
.
By Lemma
A.1.1
, relative compactness of
(\mathcal{A}_{n})
implies relative compactness of
(f_{n})
,
thus we may pass to a subsequence and assume
f_{n}\rightarrow f\in\mathcal{F}
.
Lemma
A.2.2
gives that
z\in H(f)
.
Thus
\mathcal{F}
is pervasive.
We have now shown that
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
is a deterministic weave.

Our next goal is to show that
\mathcal{F}
and
\flow(\mathcal{A})
are non-crossing.
Let
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
g\in\mathcal{A}
.
Then there exists
f_{n}\in\flow(\mathcal{A}_{n})
and
g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
such that

f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
By Theorem
4.3.9
, there exists
g^{\prime}_{n}\in\flow(\mathcal{A}_{n})

such that
g_{n}\subseteq g^{\prime}_{n}
.
We have
\flow(\mathcal{A}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
, so
by Lemma
A.1.1
the set
\mathcal{F}\cup(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\flow(\mathcal{A}_{n}))

is compact,
which implies that
\{g^{\prime}_{n}\,;\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is relatively compact.
Hence there exists
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
g^{\prime}_{n}\rightarrow g^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
A.2.2
we have
g\subseteq g^{\prime}
.
Both
f_{n}
and
g^{\prime}_{n}
are elements of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, hence they do not cross each other.
By Lemma
3.3.6
,
f
and
g^{\prime}
do not cross each other.
As
g\subseteq g^{\prime}
this means that
f
and
g
do not cross.

We now have that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{A}
do not cross,
and that both are deterministic weaves consisting entirely of bi-infinite paths.
Lemma
4.2.6
gives that
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{A}
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.4.4

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be deterministic weaves and assume that
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing.
Then
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is a deterministic weave and
\flow(\mathcal{A})=\flow(\mathcal{B})
.

Proof.

It is trivial to check that
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is a deterministic weave.
Lemma
4.4.2
gives that
\flow(\mathcal{A})
and
\flow(\mathcal{B})
are deterministic weaves,
composed entirely of bi-infinite paths.
Lemma
4.1.4
gives that a path
f\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
crosses
\mathcal{A}
if and only if it crosses
\mathcal{B}
,
so by Lemma
4.2.6
we have
\flow(\mathcal{A})=\flow(\mathcal{B})
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.4.5

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Then

\flow(\mathcal{A})
is a maximal element of
(\mathscr{W}_{\det},\preceq)

and

\mathcal{A}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

The reader may wish to check (
2.7
) for the definition of the partial order
\preceq
.
Note that Lemma
4.4.2
gives that
\flow(\mathcal{A})\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
Let us first show that
\mathcal{A}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{A})
.
Theorem
4.3.9
gives that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq(\flow(\mathcal{A}))_{\uparrow}
.
Now consider
f\in\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}\cap\flow(\mathcal{A})
.
For such
f
we have
f\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
, which implies
f\in\mathcal{A}
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{A})
.

It remains to show that

\flow(\mathcal{A})
is a maximal element of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
Lemma
4.4.2
gives that
\flow(\mathcal{A})\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{B}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
, comparable under
\preceq
to
\flow(\mathcal{A})
.
We must show that
\mathcal{B}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{A})
.
From Lemma
3.1.7
we have that
\flow(\mathcal{A})\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing.
From what we have already proved we have that
\mathcal{A}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{A})
,
so using Lemma
3.1.7
again gives that
\mathcal{A}\cup\flow(\mathcal{A})
is non-crossing.
Thus by Lemma
4.1.3
we have that
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing.
Lemma
4.4.4
gives that
\flow(\mathcal{A})=\flow(\mathcal{B})
.
From what we have already proved we now have that

\mathcal{B}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{B})=\flow(\mathcal{A})
, as required.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.4.6

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave. The set of ramification points of
\mathcal{A}
has Lebesgue measure zero.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.4.2
we have that
\flow(\mathcal{A})=\{f\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}\,;\,f\text{ does not cross }\mathcal{A}\}

is a weave.
Lemma
4.2.5
gives that the set of ramification points of
\flow(\mathcal{A})
has measure zero.
By Theorem
4.3.9
any ramification point of
\mathcal{A}
is also a ramification point of
\mathcal{F}
.
The result follows.

\blacksquare

In view of Lemma
4.4.6
, for any deterministic weave
\mathcal{A}

there exists a dense countable subset
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
that is non-ramified.
In Lemma
5.4.1
we will address, for random waves,
how such a subset may be chosen to be a random variable.

4.5
The web map

In this section we study properties of the
\web
operation defined in (
2.12
).
Some results in this section are analogues of properties that were proven
(for the
\flow
operation) in Section
4.4
.
We also address the dependence, or rather the lack thereof, of (
2.12
) on the set
D
.
The web operation involves both the ‘downset’ operation
A_{\uparrow}=\{f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}\,;\,f\subseteq g\text{ for some }g\in A\}

and taking closure in
\Pi^{\uparrow}
,
so we begin with the interaction between these two operations.

Lemma 4.5.1

If
A\subseteq\Pi^{\uparrow}
is relatively compact then
A_{\uparrow}
is relatively compact and
(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}=\overline{(A_{\uparrow})}
.

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from Proposition
A.2.1
.
It remains to establish that
(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}=\overline{(A_{\uparrow})}
.
To this end,
suppose that
f\in(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}
.
Then there exists
(g_{n})\subseteq A
such that
g_{n}\rightarrow g\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
and
f\subseteq g
.
Let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
denote the initial point of
f
.
By Lemma
A.2.2

z\in\overline{\cup_{n}H(g_{n})}
.
Hence, we may pass to a subsequence of the
(g_{n})
and choose
z_{n}\in H(g_{n})
such that
z_{n}\rightarrow z
.
It follows from Lemma
A.2.2
that
g_{n}|_{z_{n}}\rightarrow g|_{z}=f
,
so
f\in\overline{(A_{\uparrow})}
. Thus
(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}\subseteq\overline{(A_{\uparrow})}
.

In preparation for proving the reverse inclusion, let us first show that
if
B\subseteq\Pi
is compact then
B_{\uparrow}
is closed.
Take such an
B
, and let
(f_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\uparrow}
with
f_{n}\rightarrow f
.
We have
(g_{n})\subseteq B
such that
f_{n}\subseteq g_{n}
.
By compactness, and passing to a subsequence of
(g_{n})
,
we have that
g_{n}\rightarrow g\in B
.
By Lemma
3.3.3
we have
f\subseteq g
,
thus
f\in B_{\uparrow}
,
which establishes that
B_{\uparrow}
is closed.

We now show the reverse inclusion.
Suppose that
f\in\overline{(A_{\uparrow})}
.
Then there exists
(g_{n})\subseteq A
and
f_{n}\subseteq g_{n}
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
.
In particular,
g_{n}\in\overline{A}
which implies
f_{n}\in(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}
.
From the previous paragraph we have that
(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}
is closed,
so
f\in(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}
.
Thus
(\overline{A})_{\uparrow}\supseteq\overline{(A_{\uparrow})}
,
as required.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.5.2

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a deterministic weave. Then
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}
is a deterministic weave
and
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}\preceq\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Relative compactness of
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}
is given by Lemma
4.5.1
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is a weave,
\mathcal{A}
is closed, so from Lemma
4.5.1
we have that
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}
is closed,
which thus implies compactness.
The non-crossing property of
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}
is inherited from
\mathcal{A}
by Definition
2.3.1
.
Using that
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}=(\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow})_{\uparrow}

it is straightforward to check that (
2.7
) gives
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}\preceq\mathcal{A}
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.5.3

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave and
D,D^{\prime}
be dense non-ramified subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Define
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
as in (
2.12
).
Then
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})=\web_{D^{\prime}}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a weave and let
D
,
D^{\prime}
be dense non-ramified subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We note that it suffices to prove that

(\mathcal{A}|_{D})_{\uparrow}\subseteq\overline{(\mathcal{A}|_{D^{\prime}})_{\uparrow}}.

(4.24)

With (
4.24
) in hand,
by symmetry we also have that
(\mathcal{A}|_{D^{\prime}})_{\uparrow}\subseteq\overline{(\mathcal{A}_{D})_{\uparrow}}
.
Taking closures shows that
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})=\web_{D^{\prime}}(\mathcal{A})
.

Let
f
be an element of the left hand side of (
4.24
).
Then there exists
z\in D
and
g\in\mathcal{A}(z)
with
f\subseteq g|_{z}
.
Using that
D^{\prime}
is dense,
take
z_{n}\in D^{\prime}
such that
z_{n}\rightarrow z
,
and using that
\mathcal{A}
is pervasive
take
g^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{A}(z_{n})
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{A}
we may pass to a subsequence and assume that

g^{\prime}_{n}\rightarrow g\in\mathcal{A}(z)
,
which by Lemma
A.2.2
implies that

g^{\prime}_{n}|_{z_{n}}\rightarrow g|_{z}
.

Since
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(z)
and
z
is non-ramified,
we have that
g|_{z}=g^{\prime}|_{z}
.
Thus
f\subseteq g^{\prime}|_{z}
.
Let
w
denote the initial point of
f
.
Using that
g^{\prime}_{n}|_{z_{n}}\rightarrow g^{\prime}|_{z}
,
by Lemma
A.1.1
we thus have

w\in H(g^{\prime}|_{z})\subseteq\overline{\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}H(g^{\prime}_{n}|_{z_{n}})}.

If
w\in H(g^{\prime}_{n}|_{z_{n}})
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}

then we have
f\subseteq g^{\prime}_{n}|_{z_{n}}\in\mathcal{A}|_{D^{\prime}}
,
and we are done.
Otherwise,
there exists a subsequence of
n
such that

w_{n}\in H(g^{\prime}_{n}|_{z_{n}})
and
w_{n}\rightarrow w
,
and without loss of generality we pass to this subsequence.
We thus have
g^{\prime}_{n}|_{w_{n}}\subseteq g^{\prime}_{n}|_{z_{n}}
,
so
g^{\prime}_{n}|_{w_{n}}\in(\mathcal{A}_{D^{\prime}})_{\uparrow}
.
Noting that
w_{n}\rightarrow w
and
g^{\prime}_{n}\rightarrow g
,
it follows form Lemma
A.2.2
that

g^{\prime}_{n}|_{w_{n}}\rightarrow f
.
Hence
f\in\overline{(\mathcal{A}_{D^{\prime}})_{\uparrow}}
.
This establishes (
4.24
) and completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.5.4

From the point onwards we will often invoke Lemma
4.5.3
implicitly,
through writing
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})=\web(\mathcal{A})
.
Lemmas
4.4.6
and Lemma
4.5.3
combine to show that

\web:\mathscr{W}_{\det}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{\det}
is a deterministic function.

Lemma 4.5.5

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be deterministic weaves and assume that
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing.
Then

\web(\mathcal{A})=\web(\mathcal{B})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing, it is trivial to check that
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is a weave.
By Lemma
4.4.6
there exists

D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is dense and non-ramified with respect to
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
.
Note that this implies
D
is also non-ramified with respect to both
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
Fix some
z\in D
and consider
f\in\mathcal{A}(z)
and
g\in\mathcal{B}(z)
.
Note that
(\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B})(z)=\mathcal{A}(z)\cup\mathcal{B}(z)
.
We have
f,g\in(\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B})(z)
and
z
is non-ramified, so we have
f|_{z}=g|_{z}
.
Since
z,f,g
were arbitrary, by (
2.12
) this implies
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})=\web_{D}(\mathcal{B})
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.5.6

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Then
\web(\mathcal{A})
is a deterministic weave.

Proof.

Fix a dense non-ramified
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By Lemma
4.5.3
it suffices to check that
\mathcal{W}=\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
is a weave.
Noting that

(\mathcal{A}|_{D})_{\uparrow}\subseteq A_{\uparrow}
,
compactness of
\mathcal{A}
and Lemma
4.5.1
implies compactness of
\mathcal{W}
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{W}
inherits the non-crossing property from
\mathcal{A}

by Lemma
4.5.2
.
Lastly, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
there exists
(z_{n})\subseteq D
such that
z_{n}\rightarrow z
.
Take
f_{n}\in\mathcal{A}(z)
and note
g_{n}=f_{n}|_{z_{n}}\in\mathcal{W}
.
By compactness
g_{n}
has a sub-sequential limit point
g\in\mathcal{W}
,
and by Lemma
A.2.2
we have
g\in\mathcal{W}(z)
.
Thus
\mathcal{W}
is pervasive,
so we have that
\mathcal{W}
is a weave.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.5.7

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Then
\web(\mathcal{A})
is a minimal element of
(\mathscr{W}_{\det},\preceq)

and
\web(\mathcal{A})\preceq\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Let
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be non-ramified with respect to
\mathcal{A}
and
let us write
\mathcal{W}=\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
.
Note that Lemma
4.5.6
gives that
\mathcal{W}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
Let us first show that
\mathcal{W}\preceq\mathcal{A}
.
It is immediate from (
2.12
) that
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\overline{(\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow})}
,
so by Lemma
4.5.1
and compactness of
\mathcal{A}
we have
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}
.
Proposition
A.2.1
implies that
\mathcal{A}|_{D}
is relatively compact,
thus from Lemma
4.5.1
and (
2.12
)
we have

\mathcal{W}_{\uparrow}=(\overline{(\mathcal{A}|_{D})_{\uparrow}})_{\uparrow}=\overline{(\mathcal{A}|_{D})_{\uparrow}}=\mathcal{W}
,
so trivially
\mathcal{W}_{\uparrow}\cap\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
.
According to (
2.7
) we now have
\mathcal{W}\preceq\mathcal{A}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{B}
is a deterministic weave, comparable to
\web(\mathcal{A})
.
We must show that
\web(\mathcal{A})\preceq\mathcal{B}
.
The argument is analogous to that of Lemma
4.4.5
.
From Lemma
3.1.7
we have that
\web(\mathcal{A})\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing.
From what we have already proved we have that
\web(\mathcal{A})\preceq\mathcal{A}

so using Lemma
3.1.7
again gives that
\mathcal{A}\cup\web(\mathcal{A})
is non-crossing.
Thus by Lemma
4.1.3
we have that
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is non-crossing.
Lemma
4.5.5
now gives that
\web(\mathcal{A})=\web(\mathcal{B})
.
From what we have already proved we have that

\web(\mathcal{A})=\web(\mathcal{B})\preceq\mathcal{B}
, as required.

\blacksquare

5
Random weaves

We now turn our attention to random weaves.
We will give the proof of our main results
(stated in Section
2.4
)
in Sections
5.3
–
5.7
.
We require some technical matters to be dealt with first,
largely concerning measurability,
before we are in a position to rigorously work with random weaves.
The proofs in Sections
5.1
and
5.2

are not necessary for the reader wishing to understand
the proofs of our main results in later sections.

5.1
On measurability

To make sense of the statements of our main results in Section
2.4
we require that several objects are measurable.
For example, if
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are random weaves the we need
\{\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}\}
to be an event
and we need
\web(\mathcal{A})
and
\flow(\mathcal{A})
to be random variables.
In Appendix
A.3
we show that several basic maps associated to the space
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
are measurable,
whereas here we consider measurability with a focus specific to weaves.
We have also seen in Lemma
4.4.3
that the map
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\flow(\mathcal{A})
is
a continuous map from
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
to itself.
The map
\web(\cdot)
is not continuous on
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
,
as shown by example in Figure
2.5.1
.
We defined
\web
and
\flow
as maps with domain
\mathscr{W}_{\det}

but we check measurability in terms of the topology on
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
,
so the following lemma is a technical necessity.

Lemma 5.1.1

It holds that
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
is a measurable subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
.

Proof.

We have

\mathscr{W}_{\det}=\{A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})\,;\,A\text{ is pervasive}\}\cap\{A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})\,;\,A\text{ is non-crossing}\}
.
Using Lemma
A.2.2
it is straightforward to check that

\{A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})\,;\,A\text{ is pervasive}\}
is closed.
It remains to show that

\{A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})\,;\,A\text{ is non-crossing}\}

is measurable.

Consider if
A_{n},A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
are such that
A_{n}\rightarrow A
,
and
A_{n}
is non-crossing for each
n
.
Suppose that
A
fails to be non-crossing,
in particular suppose that
f,g\in A
cross each other.
We have
f_{n},g_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
By Lemma
3.1.3
we have
f_{n}\vartriangleleft g_{n}
or
g_{n}\vartriangleleft f_{n}
,
at least one of which must hold for infinitely many
n
.
It follows by part 2 of Lemma
3.3.9
that
\mathcal{A}
contains a pair of paths
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}

such that
f^{\prime}\blacktriangleleft_{\epsilon}g^{\prime}
, for some
\epsilon>0
.
Writing

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\{A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})\,;\,A\text{ is non-crossing}\},

\displaystyle M_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\{A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})\,;\,\text{there exists }f,g\in A\text{ with }f\blacktriangleleft_{1/n}g\}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\{A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})\,;\,\text{there exists }f\in A\text{ with }\sigma_{f}=-\infty\text{ or }\sigma_{f}=+\infty\}.

we thus obtain

\overline{N}=N\cup\left(\overline{N}\cap\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{1/n}\right).

By part 1 of Lemma
3.3.9
we have
N\cap M_{\epsilon}=\emptyset
, hence

N=\overline{N}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{1/n}\right).

Lemma
3.3.11
implies that
M_{1/n}\cup L
is closed, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
so noting that
L
is also closed
we obtain that
M_{1/n}=(M_{1/n}\cup L)\setminus L
is measurable.
Hence
N
is measurable.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 5.1.2

The map
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\web(\mathcal{A})
is measurable from
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
to itself.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.1.1
we have that
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
is measurable.
Recall that in Remark
4.5.4

we noted that for
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}

the value of
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
does not depend upon
D
,
provided that
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
is dense and non-ramified.
We thus write
\web(\mathcal{A})=\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
.

Let
\mu
be a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
with full support and no atoms.
Let
(z_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of independent random variables with distribution
\mu
,
on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
.
The following argument is somewhat unusual,
so let us give an outline.
We will show that the map from
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
to the law of
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
is a measurable map,
and that this law is precisely the probability measure with a point-mass at
\web(\mathcal{A})
.
The stated result then follows, using that
the map from point-mass measures, to their associated points, is a measurable map.

From Lemma
A.3.2
the map

(A,\omega)\mapsto A|_{z_{i}(\omega)}

is measurable from
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
.
It follows from Lemma
A.3.3
that

(A,\omega)\mapsto M_{n}(A,\omega)=\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A|_{z_{i}(\omega)}\right)_{\uparrow}

(5.1)

is measurable, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
as a function
M_{n}:\mathscr{W}_{\det}\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.

For all
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
and
\omega\in\Omega
we have

M_{n}(\mathcal{A},\omega)\subseteq M_{n+1}(\mathcal{A},\omega)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}
.
Lemma
4.5.1
gives that
\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}
is a compact subset of
\Pi
,
which implies that the sequence
(M_{n}(\mathcal{A},\omega))_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a relatively compact subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
.
Let
\mathcal{B}
be a limit point in
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
of
(M_{n}(\mathcal{A},\omega))
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

We aim to show that
\mathbb{P}[B=\web(\mathcal{A})]=1
.
For each
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
we have that

\mathbb{P}[(z_{i})\text{ is dense in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ and non-ramified in }\mathcal{A}]=1.

(5.2)

We write
D=(z_{i}(\omega))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Let us condition on the event in (
5.2
) occurring.
Then, if
g\in B
we have
z_{i_{n}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
f_{n}\in\mathcal{A}(z_{i_{n}})

and
g_{n}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
such that
g_{n}\subseteq f_{n}|_{z_{i_{n}}}
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g
.
It follows immediately that
g\in\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
, thus
B\subseteq\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
.
Similarly, if
g\in\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
then
there exists
z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
f_{i}\in A(z_{n})
and
g_{i}\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
such that
g_{i}\subseteq f_{i}
and
g_{i}\rightarrow g
.
Hence for all
i
there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
g_{i}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{A},\omega)
.
Thus
g\in B
and
B=\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
,
which by Lemma
4.5.3
is equal to
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
.
We thus have
\mathbb{P}[B=\web(\mathcal{A})]=1
.

Let
\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{W}_{\det})
denote the space of probability measures on
\mathscr{W}_{\det}

and let
\mathscr{P}_{0}(\mathscr{W}_{\det})
denote the closed subspace of point-mass probability measures.
Let
\delta_{A}\in\mathscr{P}_{0}(\mathscr{W}_{\det})
be the probability measure that is a point-mass on
A\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
Let
\mathscr{L}_{n}^{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{W}_{\det})
denote the law of the random variable
\omega\mapsto M_{n}(\mathcal{A},\omega)
.
From what we have proved, it follows that
\mathscr{L}_{n}^{\mathcal{A}}
converges weakly to
\delta_{\web(\mathcal{A})}

the probability measure on
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
that is a point-mass on
\web(\mathcal{A})
.

For measurable
S\subseteq\mathscr{W}_{\det}
we have

\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\mathcal{A}}(S)=\mathbb{P}[M_{n}(\mathcal{A},\cdot)\in S]=\int_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathbbm{1}_{S}(M_{n}(\mathcal{A},\omega))\,d\mathbb{P}(\omega),

from which it follows that
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
is a measurable function
from
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
to
\mathscr{P}_{0}(\mathscr{W}_{\det})
.
Hence
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\delta_{\web(\mathcal{A})}
is also measurable.
It is easily seen that the map
m:\mathscr{P}_{0}(\mathscr{W}_{\det})\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{\det}

given by
\delta_{A}\mapsto A
is continuous, and thus measurable.
Compositions of measurable functions are measurable, hence the map

A\mapsto m(\delta_{\web{A}})=\web(\mathcal{A})

is measurable.

\blacksquare

Lemma 5.1.3

The set

\{(A,B)\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}\,;\,A\preceq B\}

is a measurable subset of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}
.

Proof.

Recall the definition of
\preceq
on
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
from (
2.7
).
It suffices to show that

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(A,B)\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}\,;\,A\subseteq B_{\uparrow}\}

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(A,B)\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}\,;\,B\cap A_{\uparrow}\subseteq A\}

are both measurable subsets of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}
,
where
\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}
is equipped with the product topology and corresponding Borel
\sigma
-field.
We note that
A_{\uparrow}
is closed whenever
A\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)
is closed.
Using Lemma
A.2.2
it is straightforward to check that
the set
C_{1}=\{(A,B)\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}\,;\,\forall f\in A\;\exists g\in B\text{ such that }f\subseteq g\}
is closed.
We now move on to
C_{2}
.
To this end note that

B\cap A_{\uparrow}\nsubseteq A
if and only if
there exists
f\in A_{\uparrow}
and
g\in B
such that
g\subseteq f
and
f\notin A
.
The condition
f\notin A
is equivalent to
d_{\mathcal{K}(\Pi)}(\{f\},A)>0
which,
as
A
is closed,
is in turn equivalent to
d_{\mathcal{K}(\Pi)}(\{f\},A)\geq\epsilon
for some
\epsilon>0
.
We thus have that

C_{2}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}S_{1/n}

where

S_{\epsilon}=\left\{(A,B)\in\mathcal{K}(\Pi)^{2}\,;\,\exists f\in A_{\uparrow},g\in B\text{ such that }g\subseteq f\text{ and }d_{\mathcal{K}(\Pi)}(\{f\},A)\geq\epsilon\right\}.

Similar to above, using Lemma
A.2.2
it is straightforward to check that

S_{\epsilon}
is closed, for any
\epsilon>0
.
Thus
C_{2}
is measurable.

\blacksquare

5.2
On partial ordering of random weaves

In this section we show that

\preceq_{\rm d}
is a partial order on (the laws of) random weaves,
as defined shortly below (
2.7
).
More precisely, recall that
\mathcal{P}(M)
denotes the space of probability measures on a metric space
M
.
We have shown in Lemma
3.2.2
that
\preceq
given by (
2.7
) defines a partial order on

\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
.
In Section
2.3
we defined an extension of
\preceq
to
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow}))
,
namely if
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
valued random variables then we write
\mathcal{A}\preceq_{\rm d}\mathcal{B}

if there exists a coupling of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
.
We aim to show that that
\preceq_{\rm d}
is a partial order on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow}))
.

If
\preceq
was compatible with
(\mathcal{K}(\Pi),d_{\Pi})
,
in the sense of Definition
3.3.1
,
then we could use a classical result e.g. Theorem 2.4 in
Liggett 1985
to
obtain the extension to
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{K}(\Pi))
.
However, as we saw in Remark
3.3.2

compatibility fails in this situation.
Instead we require an original argument
that uses compactness and the precise form of (
2.7
).
We first give a preliminary lemma.

Lemma 5.2.1

Suppose that
\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
valued random variables, with the same marginal distributions,
coupled such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]=1
.
Then
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]=1.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.2.1
the metric space
\Pi^{\uparrow}
is separable,
which implies that its topology has a countable base:
there exists a family
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of non-empty open subsets of
\Pi^{\uparrow}

such that any open subset
O\subseteq\Pi
can be written as
O=\cup_{i\in I}U_{i}
for some
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.

Assume the conditions of the lemma on
\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
The proof comes in two parts, corresponding respectively to the inequalities
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset]>0
and
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset]>0
, each of which will be shown to be impossible through an argument by contradiction.

Part 1.
Suppose
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset]>0
.
The reader may wish to glance at Remark
5.2.2
,
immediately below the present proof,
for a toy example to illustrate our strategy here.
For
A\subseteq\Pi
, we write

\displaystyle A^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\{b\in\Pi^{\uparrow}\,;\,\text{there exists }a\in A\text{ such that }a\subseteq b\}.

(5.3)

On the event that
\{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}\}
,
let
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}
and let
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(d^{\prime})
be the open ball in
\Pi^{\uparrow}
of radius
\epsilon
about
d^{\prime}
.
We will now show that, almost surely,
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(d^{\prime})^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}
is empty, for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(d^{\prime})^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset
for all
\epsilon>0
.
Then, taking
\epsilon=1/n
, we have sequences
f_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{1/n}(d^{\prime})
and
g_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{1/n}(d^{\prime})^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}
,
with
f_{n}\subseteq g_{n}
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{D}
we may pass to subsequence and assume convergence
f_{n}\rightarrow d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and
g_{n}\rightarrow d\in\mathcal{D}
.
By Lemma
A.2.2
we then have
d^{\prime}\subseteq d
, so
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\uparrow}
.
We have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]=1
upon which event
\mathcal{D}_{\uparrow}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
,
so
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
which is a contradiction.
Thus, almost surely, for some (random)
\epsilon>0
, we have
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(d^{\prime})^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}=\emptyset
.
Clearly also
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(d^{\prime})
.

Let

O=\begin{cases}\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(d^{\prime})&\text{ on the event that }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

From the previous paragraph have that
O^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}=\emptyset
and with positive probability
d^{\prime}\in O
.
Since
O
is open, almost surely we may write
O=\cup_{i\in I}U_{i}
for some random
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.
The set
I
is non-empty with positive probability,
hence there is some deterministic
i\in I
such that with positive probability
d^{\prime}\in U_{i}\subseteq O
.
Let us write
U=U_{i}
for such an
i
.

On the event that
d^{\prime}\in U\subseteq O
we have that
U^{\circ}\subseteq O^{\circ}
,
which implies that
U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}=\emptyset
(because
O^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}=\emptyset
)
and
U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
(because it contains
d^{\prime}
).
Hence the event
\{U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\text{ and }U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}=\emptyset\}
has positive probability.
We thus have

\displaystyle 0<\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\text{ and }U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}=\emptyset\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\right]-\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\text{ and }U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\right]-\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\text{ and }U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\text{ and }U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\emptyset\right].

(5.4)

The second line of (
5.4
) follows because
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
have the same marginal distribution, and the other steps are elementary.

Consider when the event
\{U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\text{ and }U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\emptyset\}
occurs,
which by (
5.4
) has positive probability.
Then we have
h\in U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}
,
but
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]=1
upon which event we have
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\uparrow}
,
hence there exists
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
such that
h\subseteq h^{\prime}
.
By (
5.3
) we have that
h^{\prime}\in U^{\circ}
,
which is a contradiction to
U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
Hence in fact
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset]=0
, as required.

Part 2.
Suppose
\mathbb{P}[D\setminus D^{\prime}\neq\emptyset]>0
.
The argument is similar to Case 1 but somewhat simpler, and we will make use of Case 1 within it.
Note that we should expect an asymmetric argument due to the parity inherent in
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]=1
.
To make the comparison clear we will recycle much of our notation.

On the event
\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, take
d\in\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Suppose that
B_{\epsilon}(d)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
for all
\epsilon>0
.
Taking
\epsilon=1/n
, there exists
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap B_{1/n}(d)
.
By compactness we may pass to a subsequence and assume convergence
f_{n}\rightarrow f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
This implies
f=d
, which is a contradiction since
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Hence there exists a random
\epsilon>0
such that

B_{\epsilon}(d)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

Let
O
be equal to
B_{\epsilon}(d)
on the event
\{\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}
and
O=\emptyset
otherwise.
Thus
O\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\emptyset
and with positive probability
d\in O
.
By the same argument as in Part 1, there exists deterministic
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that with positive probability
d\in U_{i}\subseteq O
.
Thus, setting
U=U_{i}
, we have that with positive probability
d\in U\cap(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
. We have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]=1
upon which event
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\uparrow}
.
Thus, when
d\in U\cap(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
there exists
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
such that
d\subseteq d^{\prime}
, implying that both

U^{\circ}\cap(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
and
U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
From Part 1 we have that almost surely
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.
Hence with positive probability we have
both
U^{\circ}\cap(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
and
U^{\circ}\cap(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{D})\neq\emptyset
.
Thus, noting that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{D}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\cup(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle 0<\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap(\mathcal{D}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\neq\emptyset\right]

\displaystyle<\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[U^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\right].

Here, the second line follows because
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
have identical distribution and
U
is deterministic.
It follows that
\mathbb{P}[U^{\circ}\cap(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D})\neq\emptyset]>0
,
but from Part 1 we know that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}=\emptyset]=1
, so we have reached a contradiction.
This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.2.2

The proof of Lemma
5.2.1
is technical but it has a simple idea at its heart.
Consider a toy example: take two uniform random variables
X,X^{\prime}
on
S=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}

and suppose that
X
and
X^{\prime}
are coupled in a way that satisfies
\mathbb{P}[X\leq X^{\prime}]=1
.
We aim to show that
\mathbb{P}[X=X^{\prime}]=1
.
For
k\in S
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X=k,X^{\prime}\neq k]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[X=k]-\mathbb{P}[X=k,X^{\prime}=k]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[X^{\prime}=k]-\mathbb{P}[X^{\prime}=k,X=k]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[X^{\prime}=k,X\neq k].

(5.5)

Note the similarity of (
5.5
) to (
5.4
).
Taking
k=1
and using that
\mathbb{P}[X\leq X^{\prime}]=1
we obtain
\mathbb{P}[X=1,X^{\prime}>1,X\leq X^{\prime}]=\mathbb{P}[X^{\prime}=1,X>1,X\leq X^{\prime}]

which becomes
\mathbb{P}[X=1,X^{\prime}>1]=0
, thus
\mathbb{P}[X=1]=\mathbb{P}[X=1,X^{\prime}=1]
.
This is clearly a step in the right direction and is
in similar style to the more complex reasoning involving
\preceq
below (
5.4
).
The finiteness of
S
is also helpful here
whereas in Lemma
5.2.3
we must rely on second countability of
\Pi^{\uparrow}
.
We leave it for the reader to complete this toy example and deduce that
\mathbb{P}[X=X^{\prime}]=1
.

Lemma 5.2.3

The relation
\preceq_{\rm d}
is a partial order on the space of
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
valued random variables.

Proof.

We will check that
\preceq_{\rm d}
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow}))
is reflexive, transitive and antisymmetric, in turn.
Lemma
3.2.2
has already shown that these properties hold in the deterministic case
i.e.
\preceq_{\rm d}
is a partial order on
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.

By reflexivity of
\preceq
on
\mathcal{W}_{\det}
we have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{A}]=1
for any
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
valued random variable
\mathcal{A}
, so
\preceq_{\rm d}
is reflexive.
For transitivity, let us assume that
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
are
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
valued random variables,
and that we have couplings
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
and
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
and (on a possibly different probability space)
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}\preceq\mathcal{C}]=1
.
It follows that there exists a joint coupling
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
on which

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\mathcal{B}\preceq\mathcal{C}]=1
.
On the event
\{\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\mathcal{B}\preceq\mathcal{C}\}
transitivity of
\preceq
on
\mathcal{W}_{\det}
implies that
\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{C}
, so we obtain
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{C}]=1
, as required.

It remains to show antisymmetry.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow})
valued random variables
such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
and
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]=1
,
where
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
have the same marginal distribution
and
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
have the same marginal distribution.
We must show that there exists a coupling under which
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}]=1
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
have the same marginal distribution,
it follows that exists a coupling
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
such that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B},\;\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]=1
.
By transitivity of
\preceq
on
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
this means that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
.
By Lemma
5.2.1
we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}]=1
which means

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}\preceq\mathcal{A}]=1
and by antisymmetry of
\preceq
on
\mathcal{W}_{\det}
we
obtain that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}]=1
, as required.

\blacksquare

Lemma 5.2.4

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a weave.
Then
\mathcal{A}
is a web if and only if
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}
is a minimal element of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}]=1
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{A}
is a flow if and only if
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}
is a maximal element of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}]=1
.

Proof.

Let us first give the argument for webs.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a weave.
We must show that
\mathcal{A}
is a web if and only if
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}
is a minimal element of
\mathcal{W}_{\det}]=1
.
It is trivial to see that almost sure pervasiveness and the non-crossing property pass from either side of the ‘if and only if’ statement to the other side, so it remains only to handle minimality.
To be explicit we must show that for a random weave
\mathcal{A}
the following statements are equivalent:

1.

If
\mathcal{B}
is a weave and there exists a coupling between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
such that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
or
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}\preceq\mathcal{A}]=1
, then
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
.

2.

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\text{ is a minimal element of }\mathcal{W}_{\det}]=1
.

By the definition of
\preceq_{\rm d}
from below (
2.7
),
the first statement is precisely the claim that the law of
\mathcal{A}
is minimal in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{K}(\Pi^{\uparrow}))
.

Let us first show that (2) implies (1). If
\mathcal{A}
is almost surely minimal in
\mathcal{W}_{\det}
then
for any coupling of
\mathcal{A}
to another weave
\mathcal{B}
,

\{\mathcal{A}\text{ and }\mathcal{B}\text{ are comparable}\}\subseteq\{\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}\}
,
where both the left and right hand side are events.
Thus (1) holds.

Conversely, let us assume (1).
Let

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{A}&\text{ on the event }\{\web(\mathcal{A})\prec\mathcal{A}\},\\
\web(\mathcal{A})&\text{ on the event }\{\mathcal{A}\preceq\web(\mathcal{A})\}.\end{cases}

Lemmas
5.1.2
and
5.2.3
combine to show that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a random variable.
By Lemma
4.5.6
we have
\mathbb{P}[\web(\mathcal{A})\preceq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]=1
, which by (1) implies that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\preceq\web(\mathcal{A})]=1
,
so in fact
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\web(\mathcal{A})]=1
.
By Lemma
4.5.6
we thus have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}=\web(\mathcal{A})]=1
,
from which Lemma
4.5.7
gives (2).

In the case of flows we may use a similar argument,
reversing the direction of the sign of
\preceq
.
Lemma
4.5.6
is replaced by Lemma
4.4.2
, and
Lemma
4.5.7
is replaced by Lemma
4.4.5
.
We leave the details to the reader.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.2.5

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a weave.
Our results in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
justify that

\web(\mathcal{A})
and
\flow(\mathcal{A})
are random variables,
and that if
\mathcal{B}
some other random weave, coupled to
\mathcal{A}
then
\{\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}\}
is an event.
Moreover,
\preceq_{\rm d}
defines a partial order on the laws of random weaves,
via the relationship

\mathcal{A}\preceq_{\rm d}\mathcal{B}

if and only if
there exists a coupling of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
such that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
.
We will use these results freely from now on
and will not repeatedly cite them when used within the proofs.

5.3
Proof of Theorem
2.4.3

We are now ready
to establish our main results concerning random weaves,
which henceforth are simply referred to as weaves.
These results were stated in Section
2.4

and the proofs are spread across Sections
5.3
–
5.7
.
The statements of Theorems
2.4.3
-
2.4.6
consist of several (numbered) parts.
We will use bold text (see e.g. the next paragraph) to track when each part is addressed.
Most of our work in Section
4
leads towards these proofs.
We begin with Theorem
2.4.3
, of which
we prove the four statements of the theorem in turn.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a weave.

Part 1.

Let us first assume (a), that
\mathcal{A}
is a web.
By Lemma
5.2.4

\mathcal{A}
is almost surely a minimal element of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
By Lemma
4.5.7
we have
\web(\mathcal{A})\preceq\mathcal{A}
,
from which minimality implies that
\mathcal{A}=\web(\mathcal{A})
, which gives (b).
Conversely, let us assume (b), that
\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\web(\mathcal{A})
.
Lemma
4.5.7
thus gives that
\mathcal{A}
is almost surely a minimal element of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
,
from which Lemma
5.2.4
gives that
\mathcal{A}
is a web.
Thus
(a)\Leftrightarrow(b)
.

Part 2.

Again, let
\mathcal{A}
be a weave.
We will show that
(a)\Rightarrow(b)\Rightarrow(c)\Rightarrow(a)
.
Let us first assume (a), that
\mathcal{A}
is a flow.
By Lemma
5.2.4

\mathcal{A}
is almost surely a maximal element of
\mathscr{W}_{\det}
.
By Lemma
4.4.5
we have
\mathcal{A}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{A})
,
from which maximality implies that
\mathcal{A}=\flow(\mathcal{A})
, giving (b).
Now let us assume (b), that
\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\flow(\mathcal{A})
.
It is immediate from (
2.13
) that
\flow(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
,
so we have (c).

Lastly, let us assume (c), that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{B}
is a weave with a coupling to
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}]=1
.
To see that
\mathcal{A}
is a flow we must show that this implies
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}]=1
.
Using that
\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}
almost surely,
it follows from (
2.7
) that almost surely

\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}\cap\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\uparrow}
.
As
\mathcal{A}\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
we thus have that almost surely
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
.
We require the reverse inclusion, so let
f\in\mathcal{B}
.
On the almost sure event that
\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}
,
by Lemma
3.1.7
we have that
f
does not cross
\mathcal{A}
,
so by Theorem
4.3.9
there exists
f^{\prime}\in\flow(\mathcal{A})

such that
f\subseteq f^{\prime}
.
Lemma
4.2.6
gives that
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
,
which implies that
f\in\mathcal{A}_{\uparrow}\cap\mathcal{B}
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}
, as required.

Part 3.

Lemmas
4.4.5
and
4.5.7
give that

\mathbb{P}[\web(\mathcal{A})\preceq\mathcal{A}\preceq\flow(\mathcal{A})]=1
.

Part 4.

The existence claim is established by part 3 of the present proof.
It remains to prove the uniqueness claim,
which we will give in turn for webs and then flows.

Let
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
be webs
and suppose that

\mathcal{W}\preceq_{\rm d}\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\preceq_{\rm d}\mathcal{A}
.
Then there exists (pairwise) couplings such that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{W}\preceq\mathcal{A}]=1
and
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{A}]=1
.
We seek to show that
\mathcal{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
.
It follows that there exists a three-way coupling of
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}
such that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{W}\preceq\mathcal{A}\text{ and }\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\preceq\mathcal{A}]=1
.
By Lemma
3.1.7
we have that
\mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{A}
is almost surely non-crossing,
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{A}
is almost surely non-crossing.
By Lemma
4.1.3
we have that
\mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
is almost surely non-crossing.
By Lemma
4.5.5
we thus have
\web(\mathcal{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\web(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
.
By part 2 of the present proof we thus have
\mathcal{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
,
hence in particular
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
have the same marginal distribution, as required.

It remains to prove a corresponding statement for flows.
Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be flows
and suppose that

\mathcal{A}\preceq_{\rm d}\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{A}\preceq_{\rm d}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Then, as above,
there exists a three-way coupling of
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}
such that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{F}\text{ and }\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}]=1
.
By the same argument as above,
again using Lemmas
3.1.7
and
4.1.3
,
with Lemma
4.4.4
in place of Lemma
4.5.5
,
and using part 1 of the present proof in place of part 2,
we obtain that
\mathcal{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Hence in particular
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
have the same marginal distribution, as required.
This completes the proof.

5.4
Proof of Theorem
2.4.4

We require some preparatory lemmas before giving the proof of Theorem
2.4.4
.

The next lemma gives us the ability use a

deterministic
non-ramified dense set of space-time points with (random) weaves.
It is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
4.4.6
,
delayed until now because when we stated Lemma
4.4.6
we were focused on deterministic weaves.

Lemma 5.4.1

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a weave.
Then the set
\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}\,;\,\mathbb{P}[z\text{ is ramified in }\mathcal{A}]>0\}
has zero Lebesgue measure.

Proof.

Let
\ram(\mathcal{A})
denote the set of ramification points of a (deterministic or random) weave
\mathcal{A}
.
We have shown in Lemma
4.4.6

that the map
(\mathcal{A},z)\mapsto\mathbbm{1}\{z\in\ram(\mathcal{A})\}
is measurable from
\mathscr{W}_{\det}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
,
and that
\ram(\mathcal{A})
is Lebesgue null for all deterministic weaves.
By Fubini’s theorem, for any weave
\mathcal{A}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}}\mathbb{P}[z\in\ram(\mathcal{A})]\,dz=\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}}\mathbbm{1}\{z\in\ram(\mathcal{A})\}\,dz\big]=0

and the result follows.

\blacksquare

Lemma 5.4.2

Let
A\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
be non-crossing and pervasive
and let
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be dense.
Let
f,h\in A
with
f\vartriangleleft h
and suppose
t\star\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is such that
f(t\star)<h(t\star)
.
Then for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
(x,s)\in D
and
g\in A((x,s))
such that
f,h\notin A((x,s))
,
f\vartriangleleft g
,
g\vartriangleleft h
and
|t-s|<\epsilon
.
Moreover, if
\star=-
then we may take
s<t
and if
\star=+
then we may take
s>t
.

Proof.

By the càdlàg property of
f,h
and denseness of
D
, there exists
(x,s)\in D
such that

|s-t|<\epsilon
, with the desired sign for
t-s
and with
f(s-)\vee f(s+)<x<h(s-)\wedge h(s+)
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is pervasive there exists
g\in A((x,s))
.
It is immediate that
f,h\notin A((x,s))
,
and that
\{f,g,h\}
is non-crossing.
By Lemmas
3.1.3
and
3.1.4
we have
f\vartriangleleft g
and
g\vartriangleleft h
.

\blacksquare

Lemma
5.4.2
is a technical lemma used in the proof of our next lemma.
Recall that in Lemma
4.4.6
we showed that
if
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
then
\ram(\mathcal{A})
,
the set of ramification points of
\mathcal{A}
,
is a measurable and null subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{c}
.
The following lemma is stated as a result for deterministic weaves,
which avoids having to find a suitable state space for random null sets.

Lemma 5.4.3

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic weave.
Then
\mathcal{A}
,
\flow(\mathcal{A})
and
\web(\mathcal{A})

all have the same ramification points.

Proof.

Let us write
\mathcal{W}=\web(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{F}=\flow(\mathcal{A})
.
By Theorem
2.4.3
,
applied to the weave whose law is a point-mass at
\mathcal{A}
,
we have
\mathcal{W}\preceq\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{F}
.
Note that if
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{\det}
with
\mathcal{B}\preceq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

then for any
b\in B
there exists
b^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
such that
b\subseteq b^{\prime}
.
It follows that

\ram(\mathcal{W})\subseteq\ram(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\ram(\mathcal{F})
.
With this in hand it remains only to show that
for any deterministic weave
\mathcal{A}
we have

\ram(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\ram(\mathcal{W}).

(5.6)

To this end,
let
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
be dense and non-ramified.
Suppose that
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c}
is ramified in
\mathcal{F}
.
We thus have bi-infinite
f,g\in\mathcal{F}(z)
that are not comparable under
\subseteq
.
It follows that there exists
s\star\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathfrak{s}}
such that
f(s\star)\neq g(s\star)
,
and without loss of generality we may assume
f(s\star)<g(s\star)
.

•

Consider first if
s\star\geq t+
.
Take a sequence
z_{n}=(x_{n},t_{n})\in D
such that
z_{n}\rightarrow z
and
x_{n}\leq f(t_{n}-)\wedge f(t_{n}+)
,
along with a sequence
w_{n}=(y_{n},s_{n})\in D
such that
w_{n}\rightarrow z
and
y_{n}\geq g(t_{n}-)\vee g(t_{n}+)
.
It it straightforward to check that such sequences exist.
Take
f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}(z_{n})
and
g_{n}\in\mathcal{F}(w_{n})
.
By compactness, passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
f_{n}\rightarrow f^{\prime}
and
g_{n}\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
.
By Lemma
A.2.2
we have
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(z)
.
Recall from Lemma
3.1.6
that
(\mathcal{F},\vartriangleleft)
is totally ordered.
By Lemma
4.2.4
we have
f^{\prime}\vartriangleleft f\vartriangleleft g\vartriangleleft g^{\prime}
,
which implies that
f^{\prime}(s\star)<g^{\prime}(s\star)
.
By Lemma
A.2.2
we have
f_{n}|_{z_{n}}\rightarrow f^{\prime}|_{z}
and
g_{n}|_{w_{n}}\rightarrow g^{\prime}|_{z}
.
Note that
f^{\prime}|_{z},g^{\prime}|_{z}\in\mathcal{W}
by (
2.12
),
and both pass though
z
.
Since
f^{\prime}|_{z}(s\star)=f^{\prime}(s\star)<g^{\prime}(s\star)=g^{\prime}|_{z}(s\star)

they cannot be comparable under
\subseteq
.
Hence, in this case, we have that
z\in\ram(\mathcal{W})
.

•

Next, consider if
s\star\leq t-
.
We may assume that
f(u\bullet)=g(u\bullet)
for all
u\bullet\geq t+
(or else, the case above applies).
By Lemma
5.4.2
there exists
z\in D
and
h\in\mathcal{A}(z)
such that
f,g\notin\mathcal{F}(z)
,
f\vartriangleleft h\vartriangleleft g

and
\sigma_{z}<t
.
We have
f(u\bullet)=g(u\bullet)
for all
u\bullet\geq t+
, and
f\vartriangleleft h\vartriangleleft g
,
which means
f(u\bullet)=h(u\bullet)=g(u\bullet)
for all such
u\bullet
.
Since
f(t-)\leq h(t-)\leq g(t-)
we have
h\in\mathcal{A}(z)
.
The properties of
h
given in Lemma
5.4.2
guarantee that
h
is not equal to
f
or
g
,
so there exists some
v_{1}\bullet_{1},v_{2}\bullet_{2}\leq t-
such that

f(v_{1}\bullet_{1})<h(v_{1}\bullet_{1})
and
h(v_{2}\bullet_{2})<g(v_{2}\bullet_{2})
.

We apply Lemma
5.4.2
twice more, to
(f,h)
at
v_{1}\bullet_{1}
and to
(h,g)
at
v_{2}\bullet_{2}
.
We thus obtain (respectively) for
i=1,2
,
z_{i}\in D
and
h_{i}\in\mathcal{A}(z_{i})

such that

f\vartriangleleft h_{1}\vartriangleleft h\vartriangleleft h_{2}\vartriangleleft g
, with
f,h\notin\mathcal{F}(z_{1})
,
h,g\notin\mathcal{F}(z_{2})
and
\sigma_{z_{1}},\sigma_{z_{2}}<t
.
The same argument as above shows that
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{F}(z)
.
It is clear that
h_{1}|_{z_{1}}
and
h_{2}|_{z_{1}}

are both elements of
\mathcal{W}
and both pass through
z
.
To complete the proof, we will show that they are not comparable under
\subseteq
.

Suppose that
h_{1}|_{z_{1}}\subseteq h_{2}|_{z_{2}}
.
Then
\sigma_{z_{2}}\leq\sigma_{z_{1}}
,
and the fact that
h_{1}\vartriangleleft h\vartriangleleft h_{2}
implies that
h\in\mathcal{A}(z_{1})
, which is a contradiction.
Similarly we cannot have
h_{2}|_{z_{2}}\subseteq h_{1}|_{z_{1}}
,
so in this case we also have
z\in\ram(\mathcal{W})
.

This completes the proof of Lemma
5.4.3
.

\blacksquare

We are now ready to give the proof of Theorem
2.4.4
.
We prove the two parts of the theorem in turn,
with part 2 first.
Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be weaves.

Part 1.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathcal{B}
.
Theorem
2.4.3
gives that
\flow(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\flow(\mathcal{B})
,
which implies that there exists a coupling of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathbb{P}[\flow(\mathcal{A})=\flow(\mathcal{B})]=1
.
Let us write
\mathcal{F}=\flow(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\flow(\mathcal{B})
.
Suppose that
\boldsymbol{z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{c}
is finite and almost surely non-ramified in both
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
Lemma
5.4.3
gives that, almost surely,

\boldsymbol{z}
is non-ramified in
\mathcal{F}
.

We seek to show that
\mathcal{A}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\mathcal{F}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}
.
Write
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
and fix
i\leq m
.
Let
f=\mathcal{A}|_{z_{i}}
.
By Theorem
4.3.9
there exists
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
with
f\subseteq f^{\prime}
.
Therefore
f^{\prime}|_{z_{i}}=f
.
As
z
is almost surely non-ramified in
\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
,
in fact
\mathcal{F}(z_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\{f^{\prime}\}
, which implies that almost surely
f^{\prime}|_{z_{i}}=\mathcal{F}|_{z_{i}}=f
.
We thus have
\mathcal{A}|_{z_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\mathcal{F}|_{z_{i}}
.

A symmetric argument shows that
\mathcal{B}|_{z_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\mathcal{F}|_{z_{i}}
,
so in fact
\mathcal{A}|_{z_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}|_{z_{i}}
.
In particular
\mathcal{A}|_{z_{i}}
and
\mathcal{B}|_{z_{i}}
have the same marginal distribution,
so
\mathcal{A}|_{z_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}|_{z_{i}}
, as required.

Part 2.

Let us first show that (a) and (b) are equivalent.
Assume (a), that
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathcal{B}
.
Theorem
2.4.3
gives that
\flow(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\flow(\mathcal{B})
,
which implies that there exists a coupling of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathbb{P}[\flow(\mathcal{A})=\flow(\mathcal{B})]=1
.
Let us write
\mathcal{F}=\flow(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s.}}{{=}}\flow(\mathcal{B})
.
From (
2.13
) we thus have

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{F}\text{ is non-crossing and }\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{F}\text{ is non-crossing}]=1
.
Lemma
4.1.3
gives that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}\text{ is non-crossing}]=1
, obtaining (b).

Conversely suppose (b), that
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
are coupled weaves such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}\text{ is non-crossing}]=1
.
By Lemma
4.4.4
we have

\mathbb{P}[\flow(\mathcal{A})=\flow(\mathcal{B})]=1
.
In particular
\flow(\mathcal{A})
and
\flow(\mathcal{B})
have the same marginal distribution,
so
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathcal{B}
.

We will next show that (a) and (c) are equivalent.
Assume (a), that
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathcal{B}
.
Lemma
5.4.1
implies
the existence of a deterministic dense countable
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}

such that
D
is almost surely non-ramified in both
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
If
\boldsymbol{z}\subseteq D
is finite then it follows by part 1 of the present proof
that
\mathcal{A}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}
and
\mathcal{B}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}
have the same marginal distribution,
which establishes (c).

Conversely suppose (c).
Note that the (deterministic) operation
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}\mapsto\web_{D}(\mathcal{A^{\prime}})

depends only on
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}|_{D}
.
Enumerate
D=(z_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and write
\boldsymbol{z}_{m}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
.
From (c) we have that
\mathcal{A}|_{\boldsymbol{z}_{m}}
and
\mathcal{B}|_{\boldsymbol{z}_{m}}

have the same marginal distribution,
so by countability of
D
in fact
\mathcal{A}|_{D}
and
\mathcal{B}|_{D}
have the same marginal distribution.
It follows that
\web_{D}(\mathcal{A})
and
\web_{D}(\mathcal{B})
have the same marginal distribution.
By Lemma
4.5.3
we thus have that
\web(\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\web(\mathcal{B})
,
so
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathcal{B}
,
which establishes (a).
This completes the proof.

5.5
Proof of Theorems
2.4.5
and
2.4.6

We give the proof of
Theorem
2.4.6
before that of
2.4.5
,
because part 2 of Theorem
2.4.5
will be proven as a
specialization of part 2 of Theorem
2.4.6
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}
are weaves.
We prove parts 1 and 2 of Theorem
2.4.6
in turn.

Part 1.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}

and let
\boldsymbol{z}_{n},\boldsymbol{z}\in(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm c})^{m}
be non-ramified.
By Skorohod’s Representation Theorem
we may (change probability space, preserving the marginal distributions of each
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{A}
)
and assume that
\mathcal{A}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{A}
.
Let us write

\boldsymbol{z}_{n}=(z_{n,1},\ldots,z_{n,m})
and

\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
.
Due to non-ramification, for each
i
the sets
\mathcal{A}_{n}|_{z_{n,i}}
and
\mathcal{A}|_{z_{i}}

almost surely contain a single bi-infinite path,
which we write as
f_{n,i}
and (respectively)
f_{i}
.
By Lemma
A.1.1
the set
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{n}

is almost surely relatively compact.
Therefore we may pass to a subsequence and assume that

f_{n,i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s}}{{\rightarrow}}g_{i}\in\Pi^{\updownarrow}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for all
i
.
Since
\mathcal{A}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{A}
we have
g_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
.
By Lemma
A.2.2
we have
g_{i}\in\mathcal{A}(z_{i})
,
which implies that
g_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{=}}f_{i}
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}_{n}|_{\boldsymbol{z}_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{A}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}
,
on the probability space generated by Skorohod’s Representation Theorem,
which implies convergence in distribution.

Part 2.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a weak limit point of
(\mathcal{A}_{n})
, that is

\mathcal{A}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{B}
along a subsequence of
n
.
Let us pass to this subsequence, without loss of generality.
Further, suppose that
\mathcal{B}
is almost surely non-crossing.
By Skorohod’s Representation Theorem,
noting that we are interested to prove distributional properties of
\mathcal{B}
,
without loss of generality we may assume that
\mathcal{A}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{B}
.
Since
\mathcal{B}
is assumed to be almost surely non-crossing,
to show that
\mathcal{B}
is a weave we need only show that

\mathcal{B}
is almost surely pervasive.
Let
z\in\mathbb{R}_{c}
.
Almost surely, for all
n
there exists
f_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(z_{n})
.
By Lemma
A.1.1
the set
\mathcal{B}\cup\left(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{n}\right)

is compact, hence there exists
f\in\Pi^{\uparrow}
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
.
As
\mathcal{A}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm a.s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{B}
we have
f\in\mathcal{B}
.
By Lemma
A.2.2
we have
f\in\mathcal{B}(z)
.
Thus
\mathcal{B}
is pervasive,
so
\mathcal{B}
is a weave.

Suppose additionally that

\mathcal{A}_{n}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}
for all almost surely non-ramified
\boldsymbol{z}\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
.
By Lemma
5.4.1
the set

R=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,;\,\mathbb{P}\left[z\text{ is ramified in $\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}$ or $\mathcal{A}_{n}$}\right]>0\}

is a Lebesgue null subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For any finite sequence
\boldsymbol{z}
of points in
D=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus R

we have (by assumption) that

\mathcal{A}_{n}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{A}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}
.
Since
R
is null,
D
is dense in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
From part 1 of the present theorem we have also that

\mathcal{A}_{n}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{B}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}
,
which implies convergence in distribution.
Hence

\mathcal{A}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}

for all finite
\boldsymbol{z}\subseteq D
.
Theorem
2.4.4
now gives that
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathcal{B}
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
2.4.6
.

We now give the proof of
Theorem
2.4.5
,
proving each of the three parts in turn.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}_{n},\mathcal{F}
are flows.

Part 1.

Note that if
z\in\mathbb{R}_{c}
is non-ramified
then
\mathcal{F}(z)
contains only a single bi-infinite path.
With this fact in hand, the argument is essentially the same as that of part 1 of Theorem
2.4.6

(from the start of the present section)
and is left to the reader.

Part 2.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

is a weak limit point of
(\mathcal{F}_{n})
.
Lemma
3.3.6
gives that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is almost surely non-crossing.
Hence, by part 2 of Theorem
2.4.6
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a weave.
As
\Pi^{\updownarrow}
is a closed subset of
\Pi
we have
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\Pi^{\updownarrow}
almost surely,
hence by Theorem
2.4.3

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a flow.

Suppose, additionally, that
(\mathcal{F}_{n})
is tight and
\mathcal{F}_{n}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{F}|_{\boldsymbol{z}}

for all non-ramified
\boldsymbol{z}\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
.
